--- LANCASEIIRE COUNTY (LUNATIC ASYLUMS).

--- Alteration of Mode of Contribution to the Asylums Fund by Lancashire County Council and County Boroughs ; Alteration and Amendment of Lanca- shire County (Lunatic Asylums and other powers) Act 1891 and Repeal of Portions of that Act ; Re- turns by Clerks to Boards of Guardians ; Repeal and Amendment of Acts ; Costs of Act. *OTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that Application will be made to Parliament in the ensuing Session by the Mayor. Aldermen, and Citizens of the City of Man- chester, and the Mayor, Aldermen and Burgesses of the County Boroughs of Barrow-in-Furness, Blackburn, Bcotle, Burnley, Bury, Oldham, Preston, Salford, St Helens, Stockport and Warrington (hereinafter referred to as " the promoters ") for leave to bring in a Bill for the following purpoees or Some of them (that is to say)— Jo Alter the present Basis of Contribution by the Lancashire County Council and the County Boroughs as defined and referred to in the Lancashire County (Lunatic Asylums and other powers) Act 1991 (herein- after referred to as the Alt of 1891) to the maintenance of the Lancashire County Lunatic Asylums and to pro- vide for the future Contributions by the County Coun- cil and the said County Boroughs to the Lancashire Asylums Fund. To Repeal so much of the Act of 1891 as provides for the apportionment of the total amount required for the purposes of the Lancashire Asylums Board and the main- tenance of the Lancashire County Lunatic Asylums (hereinafter referred to as "the Asylums Board ' and "the Asylums" respectively) between the County and the said County Boroughs according to the respective rateable values of the County and the said County. Boroughs and particularly to repeal alter or amend sec- tions 24,25 and 34 of the Act of 1391. To amend subsection (1) of section 26 of the Act of 1891. by substituting therein the words "the first day of April in every year" in place of the words " the first day of March in every year." To give effect to certain resolutions passed at meet- ings of representatives of the Lancashire County Boroughs held j at the Town Hall, Manchester, on the 15th day of March and the 16:h day of April, 1901. To provide for the future contributions to the Asy- lums Board and to the maintenance of the Asylums by the Lancashire County Council and the said County Boroughs in each year in proportion to number and the period of residence of lunatics sent by the County Council and the said County Wsroughs respectively to be Asylums in each year. To provide for the making out by the Clerks of the Boards of Guardians in which the said County Boroughs respectively are situate of returns of the number of lunatics sent to the Asylums from the districts of the County Council and the County Boroughs respectively in each year and to prescribe a form for such returns and to provide for the furnishing of copies of such returns to the County Council of Lancashire and the said County Boroughs respectively. To provide for the inclusion in such lists of all lunatics sent to the Asylums and boarded out or sent to other Asylums or places other than Asylums by the Board. To provide for the making of annual estimates by the Asylums Board of the total amount required to be raised by contributions to the Asylums Fund and for the annual apportionment of such total amount between the County Council and the County Boroughs and to fix the date for the commencement of such contributions. To provide that the provisions of the Local Lemma Act, 1875, shall be available to the Asylums Board for the purpose of raising money under the Act of 1891. and to prescribe the period for repayment of loans so raised. To:authorise the said County Boroughs or any of them to invest any moneys in their hands standing to the credit of any sinking fund or other account in the mortgage cr debenture securities of the Asylums Board. Co amend the provisions of sections 23 and 37 sub- section (4) of the Act of 1891 and to apply those provi- sions to the intended Act. To amend so far as may be necessary for the purposes aforesaid all or some of tbe provisions of the Leeal Government Act 1888, the Lunacy Acts 1890 and 1891, I and tbe Act of 1891, and to save all rights and liabilities under any orders made by the :dommissioners appointed under the Local Government Act, 1888. To provide for paymeet and apportionment of the costs charges and expenses preliminary to and of and incidental to the preparing for and passing of the Bill among the promoters or among the promoters and such others of the said County Boroughs as may be defined by the Bill. Printed copies of the Bill will be deposited in the Private Bill Office of the House of Commons on Or before the 21st day of December nest. Dated this day 15th day of NoVensber, 1901. ROBERT E. FOX, Town Clerk, Blackburn, Solicitor for the Bill ; TAHOURDINS le HITCHCOCK, 20 Victoria-street, Westminster, Parliamentary Agent&

LADIES' LETTER

My DEAR NELLTE. The camellia is one of the favourite flowers for bats where fur toques are the order of the day. A sable toque has a lovely lace scarf draped round it, and looped high over a bunch of bright red camellias with their glossy green leaves. A mix- ture of fur and very light and summerlike materials characterises most of the hats. Ermine and tulle and a lovely embroidery, composed of painted batiste applied on finest putty-coloured chiffon, and sewn round with palest gold thread, are the ingredient of one of the most successful toques. Another hat is chiefly made of cream- coloured lace, with creamy camellias and sprays of mignonette as trimming. Voilets are as popular as ever, and looking particularly well on brown or tan-coloured felt hats. A charming toque was black crinoline sewn under the raised brim, with tucks of white chiffon and lines of narrow black velvet. The top was trimmed with white chiffon, black feathers, white and pink camellias, and a knot of black velvet. Lace is sometimes brought over the brim of a hat in a way that suggests the " fall " that was in fashion 30 years ago, and soon became so common, carried out in cheap laces, that it disappeared from the well-dressed classes in a very short time. There is no doubt that it is very becoming when well at ranged and not too stiff. Painted quills are 88 much used as ever in millinery, but the atrocity of beaded and sequined quills seems to be over.

I think that blouses are certainly prettier than they have ever been before and give no signs of that extinction which is pr 'dieted to be in store for them. To take some recently noted as ex- amples: The first is in eaot mauve velvet, with front of guipure, straped across with taffeta bands; a sable tail cc Liar goes round the neck, each end fastened by s guipure medallion. The top of the sleeve is fitted to the shoulder by a lace epaulet. On another figure is a blouse of reseda silk transversely tucked all over the bodice and sleeves, the latter falling away from under-sleeves of embroidered net. The front is fastened by rings of dull gold cord over a white silk insertion. A nice blouse is in cream satin, the loose fronts opening over a tucked yoke and vest and under- sleeves of opaline mousseline. A small black velvet collar falls en the shoulders, to match tabs of black velvet, and appliques of black lace run down the fronts and bar the shoulder seams.

Corduroy is again in great Favour as a material for warm winter costuaies. It has much to recom- mend it, being comparatively inexpensive in some of the qualities, very durable, and also in point of colour to be highly approved. ,It has warmth, but is sometimes rather heavy. The class of colours in which it is usually seen may be called fur tones, such as the golden brown of sealskin, the warm, rich btown of sable, the less pronounced brown of mink, the beautiful brown-greys of beaver and otter, and the a ys of chinchilla, squirrel, and opossum.

Fur is used as an edging Lr many velvet anti cloth toques. sable and iti cheaper al!imi—marten, mink, and so forth—appearing to be most popular for the purpose, although chinchill& combines very nicely with both deep violet and mulberry colour. For everyday wear the wide-brimmed sailor shape in rather soft felt is a favourite. The crown is moderately low, and the trimming, in most cases, consists of a large bow of thick soft ribbon, and one or two wings or a cock's plume placed in front. Striped ribbons are often used, especially in black and white, on black felts. Tartan ribbons, too, are seen on a good number of felt hats, and, if well chosen, have a stylish effect. The ribbon generally chosen is a soft twilled one, and is rather •broad.

There are soma forms of jewellery that never become stale though from time to time they are ushered into obscurity for a while. The ropes of pearl that were so popular a year or two ago still. remain in vogue, but there are signs in plenty or the decline of the barbaric chain, which has been decidedly over-much worn, but quaint pendants are in great demand, and hang from necklets that reach midway between the throat and waist. The prettiest litthi buttons are stories, translucent or opaque, set in rims of gold, and furs are brightened with curious mosaic buttons or great cabochons hanging from little chz:ins. A very charming and mysterious effort is produced by the gleam of ruby and emerald globes, apparently unfastened, amongst the laces and the rich fur of a pelerine. Your loving Jr.:rm.&

CHICKLN3' LIVERS when roasted with the fowl are often dried up hard. Here is a more advan- tageous way ot using them. Dust the livers with pepper and salt, and grill them over a clear fire, first brushing the gridiron with oiled butter. Make Borne nice rounds of toast, butter it plentifully, and cut into small squares or rounds, and serve the oars' tin it..

THE MOST NUTRITIOUS.

E P PS'S

GRATEFUL-COM F 0 RTIN G.

COCOA

aREAKEAST--SUPPER.

WOMEN'S CHAI.

Within the memory of all but the oldest, the ninth of November has been the occasion of a Royal festival, so popularly known as the Prince of Wales' birthday. It was therefore most appropriate that His Majesty should have seleeted that day,for raising his aen to the familiar and time-honoured dignity of Prince of Wales. Since the title has been in abeyance there has always appeared to be something amiss, and the Royal House will seem more complete now that we once again have a Prince and Princess of Wales. In a few weeks we shall have Christmas with UJ and meanwhile it is gratifying to hear that there has been a hearty response to appeals for presents and little com- forts for the troops in South Africa. The Queen Con- sort is sending out a large number of silver mounted pipes, stamped with the Royal crest. Many dis- tinguished people are also making special efforts, and amongst them the widowed Lady Wauchope who is sending out a present for every man in the Black Watch, her gallant husband's regiment. Even the much abused War Office is once more showing that it is not utterly lest to sentiment, as it is again sending out 150,000 plum puddings, to present Tommy Atkins with a little sensor able fare at Christmas time. At the instance of the Colonial Secretary the Board of Education invites the services of a hundred compe- tent women teachers for the Boer children in the Refugee Camps. It has been frequently stated that the Boer women are even more embittered against the British than the men, and no doubt there must be some rough experiences attached to life in the camps. But Lord Milner states emphatically that the teachers would be most kindly received by the Boers, by bcth parents ancWiildren. The terms offered are £lOO salary, for one year, with board and lodging. It seems an excellent opportunity for capable and competent women who have the courage to go out, and especially for those who would go with an idea of settling in South Africa, but if not the passage home will be paid for those teachers who wish to return at the end of the year.

Since women and girls have taken so generally to earning their own living, they have given less thought to colonising the Empire, though, with their greater experience, they are much better fitted for their work than those of former generations. In Canada and South Africa the demand for really capable women is just as strong as ever, and those who have the courage to go, are sure of meeting with infinitely better chancre than are, as a rule to be found at home. There is also the less reason for prejudice against going abroad, since increased facilities for communication, and regular mails, have made the severances of old ties quite un- necessary. Present choosing is in full swing, and the difficulty with She majority of us is to find suitable gifts for our men folk, gift that will be useful as well as ornamental. This year matters promise to be easier for us in this direction, but that should not tempt us to put off the selection until the last moment, when the best of every- thing will be gone. For those whose purses are elastic, there are silver cigar boxes, with perfectly plain lids, whereon one can nave inscribed the monogram, crest,or facsimile autograph of the recipient. Then there are small cabinets in various polished woods, containing drawers, labelled "Lettersanswered," " Letters Unanswered," " Bills Paid," " Bills Unpaid," All drawers are of the same dimensions, which is • mis- take on the part of the designer. For " Bills Unpaid " and " Letters Unanswered" many men would require a little; extra space. The new match bowls will find , favour among those who object to the odour of tobacco, I In the lower part of the globe is a space for the purpose of holding rose water. At one side there is a circular hole through which the scent escapes, and in this the cigar ash is placed. The fernier overpowers the latter and renders the apartment more habitable, from a non- smoker's point of view. Reports come from Paris that men are to vie with women in wearing conspicuous colours, such as violet, red, and pink waistcoats ! Possibly that beautifully- staged play " The Last of the Dandies" which Mr Tree has produced at Her Majesty's Theatre, has helped to revive the varoties of the early days of Dickens,Dieraeli, and Bulwer Lytton. Young Disraeli, we are told by Lord Dalling, used semetimes to astonieh the friends of his youth by appearing in green velvet trousers, a canary-coloured waistcoat, and equally conspicuous crat. We are not likely to see such eccentricities again, but the merging of the masculine fancy waistcoat into the highly coloured garment, is a distinct advance in the vanities of dress, which are supposed to be woman's exclusive sphere and prerogative. Mr. Ruskin used to say that except in a pretty,grace- ful way, no woman csn draw, but he made an excep- tion with regard to Miss Kate Greenaway, who died the other day, at the comparatively early age of fifty-five. Miss Greenaway was a member of the Royal Institute of Painters in water-colours, and was an exhibitor at the Royal Academy, but it was ;largely to her drawings of children, which were her favourite subjects to which we owe the great improvement in children's style of dress, in rccent years.lNct only did the originate the well-known " Kate Greenaway" 'nodes but she designed the pictures for many popular and instructive books. _ .

A more thoroughly readable, and well-written story than Mrs B. M. Croker's " Anzel " (Methuen and C 0.,) it would be difficult to find. Whether ths scenes of her novels are laid in India. where many years of her life have been passed, or in 'he Emerald Isle, where she was horn, Mrs. Croker is equally at home, and in this 1" sketch in Indian Ink " her characters • .are as usual,

• alive, and 'every scene and every incident, bears the impress of truth. At the present time this distinguished novelist is engaged on another Irish story, the publica- tion of which, her many admirers will eagerly await. The final tcuch to an up-to-date costume, is an ex- cessively wide and spreading bow of tulle, which should appear at the throat, a little on the left side. To be I correct, the tulle should be white, but some there arel who prefer this spotted with black chenille. Others select black tulle, spotted with white, but though more economical, it is by no means such smart wear. Very fine white net does not look so well as tulle of course, but it is more durable, and the process of washing and ironing renders it as fresh and crisp as new—a con- sideration when one surveys the wreck one foggy after- noon will make of a bow of tulle. e The woman who values ner hair would do well to con- sider before investing in the all fur tcque or hat, so fashionable at the morntnt. However well built and ventilated, it is weighty and far from cool wear, and this is indeed an item to consioer when we think of the many hours our headgear rests upon our heads, during each day. Many women never remove it, and if so, only to effect a change, frcrn quite early in the morning to late in the afternoon. Even with the lightest and coolest of hats and tcques, such a proceeding is disastrous to the hair, and a point should be made of removing them whenever possible. Violets are very favourite blooms, and appear on much of the cloth, and fur and chiffon headgear just now. Other flowers employed by leading milliners, are the small dahlia, and the camellia,but the most fashion- able bloom of all is the edelweiss, clusters of whith are mixed with tulle, or fur. Clusters to match also appeal in the tulle bow, or fur boa, and again in the muff. Gold braid glitters on many costumes built in shades of brown. Gold velvet peeps out from under hats and toques, and gold ribbon is used for the twisted loops, worn with evening dress, in the hair. When the tresses are of a warm ruddy colour, the gold lights it up, and suits it remarkably well. The gold roses, Once so fashion- able have disappeared, and one can scarcely regret this, as the effect was often tawdry in the extreme. _

MADGE.

CONTINUAL CURES IN LANcesntrm.----Every week fresh evidence is reported in the newspapers concerning the wonderful cores affected in Lancashire by Dr Williams' pink pills for pale people. These pills have cured paralysis, locomotor ataxy, rheumatism, and sciatica ; I also all diseases arising from impoverishment of the blood, scrofula, rickets, chronic erysipelas, consumption of the bowels and lungs, at eemis, pale and sallow com- plexion, general muscular weakness, less of appetite. palpitations, pains in the back, venous headache and neuralgia, early decay, all forms of female we simess I and hysteria. These pills are a tonic not a purgative. They are genuine only with the full name. Dr Williams pink pills for pale people, and are sold be chemists, and by Dr Williams' Medicine Company, 46,Holborn Viaduct London. 8.C., at 23 9d a box, or six boxes for 133 9d. Pink Mils sold loose or from glass jars are not Dr Williams' pink pills accept them only in the pink closed wrappers as above described. On this pace will be found details of a rsmarkable cure at Blackburn.

THE EXAMINER, Filth Y NOVEMBER 15, 1901

INTERESTING BAND CONTEST AT

RUNCORN.

On Saturday afternoon at the Runcorn Public Hall an interesting band contest took place under the auspices of the Runcorn Cycle Club. There was • crowded attend- ance. The proceeds were in aid of the Cottage Hospital Fund. The contest was an entirely new departure and proved in every aspect to be an unqualified success. The Secretary, Mr Booth, is to be congratulated on his energy, and, owing to his exertions, backed by an ' equally hard-working committee, there is every reason to believe that the Contest will be but, the 1 forerunner of an annual institution. There were no 'less than eight entries including the following bands : Runcorn Volunteer Band,conductor Bandmaster T Outh- waite ; Frodsham Vol unteer Band, conductor, Band- master T Knight ; Preston Brook Band, conductor, Mr Arthur Withenshaw ; Runcorn S. Edward's Band, conductor Mr T Hynes ; Weston Point Works Band, Bandmaster G Robinson ; Helsby Band, conductor Mr C Bate ; Newton Wesleyan Band, conductor Mr John Brady ; Moulton Verdin Institute Band, conductor Mr T Hodkineor. The adjudicator was Mr J Harper,Widnes, and the first prize was a handsome silver cup ; 2ed prize, £l;3rd prize,los ;best cornet soloist, a gold centre nude!. The test piece was H. Round's "Song of other days." The Chairman was Mr Samuel Tayler, and he was supported by Mr and Mrs H Congreve,Mr and Mrs F J Norman, the Rev F Chambers, and several of the Cycle Club rfficials, The contest commenced at 3 o'clock and did not finish until six o'clock. Preparatory to the bands playing, the adjudicator was locked Ira a room on the stage where he could bear but could not see the bands as they came on the stage to play, so that abso- lute fairness was guaranteed. The playing,on the whole was very good, each band as it stepped on and off the platform being accorded a hearty round of applause from its respective followers. As soon as the last band had ceased playing, Mr Booth (secretary) unlocked the door and Mr Harper was brought forward on to the stage amid an outburst of ;cheerily, and said he was very pleased to find that so many people had gathered there that afternoon in support of so worthy an object. He hoped that the time was not far distant when they would have a Cottage Hespital. Be had no doubt but all of them bad mace up their minds as to which was the winning band. As there was only one judge he had listened very attentivey indeed and if the people had been in his room he could have pointed out where mis- takes had been made. The bands would persist in play- ing out of tune. They played too loud and strained everything. That was the fault more or less with every one of the bands. Some of the cornets did very well but they would persist in getting away and losing them- selves. When they should perhaps have been playing a little lullaby, they permitted themselves to run wild and shouted. He awarded the first prize to the band which played second. (Applause). The second prize to the band which played fourth. (Applause). The third prize to the one which had played seventh. (Applause). The winners in the order of merit were 1, The Run- corn Volunteer Band ; 2, Preston Brook Band ; 3, Wes- ton Point Works Band.

_ The Chairman then said that the band contest was a 13897 institution in Runcorn which be hoped would be continued for many years to come. He was specially pleased that a Runcorn band had wen and hoped they would win it neat year for it could not be theirs unless they.won it twice successively. He (the speaker) would take care that they were not short of funds for a second contest.He was sorry there were not prizes for the eight bands but he hoped it would have the effect of spurring them on to run the winners as closely as possible next year. He was sure the Runcorn people would look for- ward to neat year. He was p!eaaed to introduce Mrs Congreve to them to give the prizes to the winners. Mr Outhwaite, on behalf the Runcorn Volunteer Band then received the cup amid great applause. In turn Messrs.. A. Withenshaw, G Robinson, W Gregory and L Pye received their prizes amid the plaudits of their res- pective followers. Votes of thanks to Mrs Congreve and the Chairman concluded the meeting.

BOTTLED BEER TRADEMARKS. Important Case at bt. Helens,

Liverpool Agent Smartly Fined.

A case of considerable interest to the bottled beer trade was heard at the St Helens police court,on Monday,wten Edwai d Horrigan, of 31, Islington, Liverpool, district manager of the Pt °mix Brewery Company, Dublin, was summoned under the Merchandise Marks Act with frau- dulently using the trade marks of other firms on bottles of stout and beer. The firms concerned were Messrs Samuel Allsopp and Sons; Messrs J F Eddisbury and Co. ; Messrs Greene'', Whitley, and Co. • Tbrelfall's Brewery Company ; and Messrs William Preston and Sour. Mr F E Smith, barrister, instructed by Mr H L Riley, St Helens, appeared to prosecute on behalf of the Liverpool and District Mineral Water and Ale and Porter Bottlers' Association ; and Mr F A Greer, barrister, in- structed by Meseta Oakkott, Baxter, and Chevalier, of Liverpool, represented the defendant. Mr Smith stated that the complainant was Mr Frederick Bastow, the inspector of the Liverpool and District Manufacturi rs and Bottla'rs Association which was an important erganisation with tumerous member- ship and branches over the country. It existed for the purpose of protecting the interests of members. Mr Bastow had discovered that Horrigan bad been in th,- bab't of eupplying bis customers with beer in the beltles of otter manufacturers. Great importance wa e attributed to the case because the act of bottling beer and stout was almost as important as the manufacture ,of it. In [genie cases an adhesive label bad been put 'over the indelible trademark, hut there was no-guaran- tee that this labe.l would not come off before it reached the retailer.

Frederick Bastow, the inspector, gave evidence that he had purchased three bottles of stout from James Lloyd, licensee of the Liverpool Inn, St Helens, on Sep- tember 281 b. They were Allsopp's moulded bottles,but bore the laoel of the Penis Brewery. The associa- tion had a clearing house and did all they could to see that members got their own bottles, and to see that every brewer bought a proper supply of bottles to con- duct his own business.

James Lloyd, licensee of the Liverpool Inn, said that he purchased the steut from the Pt curds Brewery Com- pany. In answer to Mr Greer, he said a customer was more likely to go by the paper label than by the stamped name of Allsopp's on the bottle. Charles Boldiag,manager of the Liverpool bottling de- partment for Messrs Alisopp, said they did not approve cf otber people using their bottles. He had seen lots of bottles come back with the Pt eenix Brewery label on them, but the bottles mostly belonged to other firme. Messrs Alsopp objected to finding bottles fur other vople to carry on their business witb. The defence was that it was a common practice for a bottler to take anyb3d3 bottle, from a licensed vic- tualler and fill actin with his own Equor when supply- ing that licensed victualler,and to put his own labels on the bottles. The customers were not deceived thereby, and there had been no intention to detraud,or any fais.i application of any trade mark. The maghtrates ultimately decided to convict and imposed a line of £l5 and £lO costs, in the first cases. The other cases were withdrawn.

A QUEEN'S SOLDIER.

(Copyright.)

1

By WALTER WOOD, Author of "Barrack and Battlefield," "Famous British Warships," "Through Battle to Pro- motion," "A Captain in the Fusiliers," "A Soldier of the Legion," So,

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: Mark Benn, the son of a substantial farmer in Yorkshire, volunteers for servioe in the Yeomanry, about to be despatched to Soilth Africa. After the farewells due to his father and his sweetheart, Lizzie Jackson, he duly embarks, the troop also including his friend Brigg. To his surprise he finds Lizzie Jackson at Capetown as an army nurse, she having left England on a mail steamer, which had passed the slow-going transport. Bean and Brigg are in a skirmish with three Boers. Poor Brigg is shot through the head, but not before he has wounded one of the Boers, and Benn distinguishes himself by disarming and taking the three prisoners. In a skirmish some days later Bonn is twice wounded, and is himself taken prisoner. He swoons, and on recovering his senses finds himself in a Boer farmhouse, where he is kindly treated. The next day he is moved to Pretoria.

• 'CHAPTER XI. IN CAPTIVITY. Benn had not been a prisoner for long when he learnt that even if there should be an exchange of prisoners, and he had the chance to take the field again, there was no hope that he would be able to fight, either mounted or afoot; and from that imoment he determined that he would watch for his 'chance and get away from the capital. The wound to his shoulder had soon got well; had been a mere matter of time—long enough to allow the lacerated flesh to heal; but the bullet- wound in the leg was different. This was a stubborn injury, one that refused to improve enough - to enable Benn to get about. The bullet remained in the leg—it had gradually worked behind the knee, and the doctors had refused to trouble with it further for the present. So the missile'remained. in its place, and Mark was left pretty much to his own devices until he was able to leave hospital and hobble about. From the hospital Mark was transferred to the aptured British soldiers' quarters at Waterfall, some distance from the town. Here he found that the prisoners lived in dwellings which had been built in such a way that they formed three long rows, or streets. These temporary gaols were surrounded by barbed wire fences, and the 'care of the captives was entrusted to the burgher guard. To Mark, country-bred as he was, it seemed as if the human beings were treated in pretty much the same fashion as poultry were cared for at home. They had their dwellings to retire to, and were kept from straying by wire fences. He chafed at the degradation of the imprisonment, and became possessed with the determination to escape. There were, too, many indignities and annoyances to endure which it was especially hard for a man of free spirit like hirinielf to bear. There were robberies from prisoners who could not help thems selves, and an amount of needless suffering, arising from the neglect of the captors, which seemed to Mark disgraceful in an enemy claiming to be civilised. There were insanitary surroundings, a lack of medical attention and comforts, and a very poor diet. A smaller matter was that for a long period no soap was issued, so that neither Mark nor his companions could maintain even the personal cleanliness which was essential to health. Dysentery was rife, and many a soldier whom Mark had seen in the prison enclosure disappeared, to be seen no more. He did not pretend to be a military expert, but it did seen' to him that here was wanton disregard of simple duty to himself and fellow-prisoners. A month after his arrival at Pretoria, Mark could walk slowly with the help of a thick stick ; but only for a very little distance, and then with pain and difficulty. Remote as was the chance of his escape, he was inces.antly guarded and as elaborately watched as if tie was in the most vigorous health and able to perform feats of wondrous agility. With the rest of his comrades who had been captured, Mark was kept a prisoner at Waterfall. Occasionally they heard something of the manner of life that the British officers led who were lodged in the &auto Model School—a modern building of red brick, lighted by electricity. These more fortunate captives could buy meat and drink, and get their cheques honoured at the Standard Bank; and as they had orderlies, and were allowed to exercise freely in the covered flagged walk outside the building, it seemed to the less favoured prisoners, who had no means of bettering their condition, that they were in very enviable case. But so far as their food and drink went, Mark could have managed well enough. It was the captivity which weighed upon him with depressing force. The soldiers chafed under its monotony, and daily grew more intolerant of the restraints which their captors put upon them. The bitter humiliation, too, of the position was not forgotten —by Mark least of all, though he was surprised to notice with what calmness some of the captives reconciled themselves to their lot, if they were prisoners, they reasoned, without too much in the way of comfort and enjoyment, they were at any rate in better case than those who had to fight against the unspeakable privations of the war, the perils of the battlefield, and the greater dangers of disease. "I tell you," said Wade, frankly, one day when he and Mark were lounging in the enclosure, "if it comes to that, I thank God I'm out of it. I've had enough of it already. I didn't want a beanfeast exactly; but all the same, I didn't want what we were getting let .in for. We came out with such a flourish of trumpets that I thought we were certain to be looked after all right, if nothing else. But see what's happened—men have gone down like rotten sheep, and the horses got out of condition so much that they were only fit for the knacker's yard. If things have been so bad so soon in the war, isn't it reasonable to suppose that they'll be a sight worse later on?" "That's all very well," said Mark, impatiently; "but war's war. Fighting and sickness, so it seems, are bound to come. It's this nailing that I object to. It sickens me.ptararWtp eoufscaawsnarn theyetfsedrsi:defio,nr,geisyse o u, Lsletl,vse si ifiosrt lase t timn g u eh thema anyway,' said Wade. "five and take—we bag some of their lot, they bag some of ours." "I don't know about our bagging them," said Mark, bitterly ; "but this place is pretty well glutted with British. You'd think from the look of it, that so far all the taking's been on their side, and no giving." • "Fortune of war," said Wade, stolidly. "Besides, they've got so many Regulars pinned up here that I don't see why we—only Yeomen— should be disturbed. If it doesn't disgrace them, I don't see why it should us." "Only Yeomen ! " echoed Mark.l"Same old story I Even here there seem to be those who think that we're only playing at soldiers. I tell you this, as soon as ever I can manage it, I'm going to get back to the fighting line. It's for us to shew that we're just exactly as good soldiers as anybody else." "But you can't manage to get back to the .fighting line," said Wade, placidly. :,"A bird might as well try to get out of its cage." "Birds sometimes do," answered Mark. "And get killed for their pains," observed Wade. "I'd rather be killed than go on like this, protested Mark. "Well, that's where we differ, Berm. The living Yeomen here mayn't be the finest of fellows; but anyway they're a dashed sight better off than having their bones picked on the veld. If I get back to the company in proper order, well and good; if not, I shall just stick here till I go away in the natural course of things. You take my tip, arid make your mind up that you'll do the same." "Not I," growled Mark. "I'll keep my eyes open, and cut at the first chance. You seem to think a lot of this place—what price the disease ?" "It's a good thing you've got a gamnay leg," said Wade, ignoring the question, and by way of ending the talk. "There's no telling what folly you'd go in for if you could get about on your pins all right. Now, don't you worry yourself any more —the worst of you quiet chaps is that when you do break out, you're like volcanoes." He had meant that this should end the conversa- tion, and had already turned his back upon Benn, when he suddenly faced him again, and with an admiring guffaw exclaimed : "By George! But you're a cute young dog ! I shouldn't have thought yoted got it in you. I see through it now as clear as daylight." • "Through what ?" asked Berm, bewildered. "Why, 'your game, of course," said Wade, with a joyous laugh. "I'm a chucklebend not to have thought of it before. it isn't hack to the front you want to get—it's to the girl—that girl of yours Tot saw on the doe): side."

"I —I--" stammered Mark. "Now then, don't deny it! It won't wash,* exclaimed Wade. "Besides, what's the good of saying it isn't true, with a face like yours? Confess—isn't that the real truth? Aren't you sick for the sight of her ?" "I should like to see her," Mark admitted. Bronzed though his face was, it went a deeper hue 1 as he said the words. He had been clever enough to conceal his real purpose, but was too simple, ' when directly charged, to get out of what he took to be his difficulty. The simple truth was that while arguing with his comrade, he really did believe that his chief reason for wishing to escape was to rejoin his company and get back to the front; but strong as duty and further service were, love and Liz were stronger. "I knew it! "continued Wade, triumphantly. "I felt it in me, only I couldn't put it into words. And so you're really that bad after her, are 70P r I thought you were of the don't-care sort; but at'. waters run deep. Well, she's a jolly plucky girl to come out, and I'll be bound she'll be worth more than a dozen of the swell loafers put together; but, all the same, you'll be a madman to try and get away from here before your time. Even suppose you gave 'em the slip, how are you to hobble on with a leg like yours?" "I shouldn't mind the pain," growled Mark, who, now that his secret was discovered, felt a strange pleasure in being able to discuss his plan freely; besides, it was an intense joy to speak of Lizzie, even with a hopeless bachelor like Wade. "Now, look here," said Wade, in the manner of an experienced person giving advice and warning to a raw youth, "I'll put the case before you in a nutshell. You try to escape, you're seen, and you're shot down. "But I'm not bound to be seen," persisted Benn. "This place isn't so sharply watched that a man can't get away." "All right," said Wade, readily. "You get past the guards and the outposts, you're not shot at; you're strong enough to make your way on to the veld. You've had pluck and energy enough to get you through so far, also grub enough to keep you alive. You fall down on the earth for a nap—when you wantko get up and walk, you find that your leg gives you such agony that you can't stand. What then ? "

Wade paused dramatically, expecting Berm t.. 7". complete the picture. "It's your story, not mine," said Mark, quietly. "The crows get you," said Wade, clinchingly. "All right, they'll be welcome to me," answered Mark. "But I'll tell you this, I'd pull myself along if I could stand at all, no matter what the pain was—not even if it was enough to make me sweat blood."

For a long minute Wade stood and stared at his companion. Then he said: "I thought of calling you a donkey, because I fancied you were mulish, but I won't. You're one of the right sort, an' I hope you'll null through and meet the girl. Remember me -to her. I liked her look, as far as I saw it. Here, put that in your pocket, you'll find 'em soothing when you're a bit down in the mouth. They're the sort of thing we'd only use at home to raise a nuisance, but here things are different." He produced a carefully-preserved packet of cheap cigarettes—ten for twopence retail, at home—and offered it to Mark. "There's the matches, too. I shan't want 'em," added Wade. He spoke as if he were doing a mere matter-of- fact deed, whereas he was actually offering, as a gift, his most valued treasure. Benn's eyes glistened. Instinctively his hand went out to take the gift, almost priceless now. But he withdrew his hand and checked his eagerness. "No, I won't—l won't rob you," he said, jerkily. "I know you refused a sovereign for 'era yesterday, and that they're as dear to you as they could be to me."

"Take 'em," growled Wade. "Let's have no fuss about a trifle."

"I can't," answered Mark. "I know you've been keeping them back, so that you could have a special treat." "If you don't," his comrade threatened, "I'll crumble the lot up and scatter 'em to the winds of heaven."

He cast the packet and the box at Mark, and with muttered thanks Benn, seeing that further protest was useless and knowing that Wade would carry out his threat, caught them both and put them in a pocket. "I'll not forget this," he said. "Come," said Wade, who disliked either giving or accepting thanks, "it's time we shuffled in. People will suspect us of something if we go on yarning here." They had been talking in an open space well away from fellow prisoners and guards, an open space in which they were kept as much by the barbed wire fences as by rifles, and across this they now wilked slowly back to their prison, chatting indifferently. On every hand, it seemed to Wade, there wero insuperable difficulties in the way of an escape— watchful gaolers, cruel fences, locks and bars and bolts. They were, too, in the heart of a hostile country, where all the rivers, roads and railway,' were held by vigilant people. It seemed such sheer madness even to think of trying to escape that involuntarily lie turned to Benn as they reached the building, and was about to warn him again of the folly of a trial. But he remembered what had just passed between them, and:with a sigh he led the way into the prison. There was a hubbub of voices as they entered.

In the commotion what seemed like an inspira- tion came to Wade. lie turned to Bonn and whispered hoarsely: "When you're ready let me know. I'll go with you. After all I'm just about as sick of this as you can be."

CHAPTER XII.

MARINO READ-r 4

+lark did not answer. Curiously enough, he was ~orry that his friend shmild have come to this determination. He was alive to the perils of his undertaking, and knew that, even if he got clear of Waterfall and the capital, the chances were that he would never reach his own people. For this reason he did not wish to have a companion; least of all did he desire Wade, since that trooper had expressed himself in such strong terms against the scheme, and had been so sure of the impossibility of escape. For the rest of the day Mark pondered over the matter. During the night he ' lay awake and thought about it still, and by morning he had quite made up his mind as to what he should He would be perfectly frank with Wade, tell ffsa: that the venture was so perilous that he could not allow him to have a part in it, and at all costs insist upon carrying out his scheme alone. lie made all this clear to Wade after the scanty breakfast. To his vast relief his comrade took the news of the decision very easily. He also had been thinking; lie had, too, suffered in the darkness from fearful visions of the fate of Benn and him- self, and had resolved that while he would keep to his word to go with Mark, if necessary, he would, if the chance came of withdrawing honourably from the position, take it. Mark's behaviour made the task of Wade quite easy. It also enabled him to make some show of insisting upon sharing the perils with him. But Mark refused to listen. "The idea's mine," he said, "and I won't drag anybody in the bother with me. If the thing really is a fizzle, it will be better for:one man only to go under than two." "Of course," said Wade, with feigned reluctance to abandon his position, "it isn't as if I'd a girl waiting for me at the other end. If I were in your shoes, I'd do the same. I couldn't help it." "I'm sure of it," said Mark. "Noz," continued Wade, anxious to satisfy his own conscience in the matter, "it isn't as if .I felt this gaol-life specially hard. I think we're pretty well looked after—as well as anyone can expect t. be ; and there's lots to interest us. You ' a bit of a student of human nature."

But there, the Lieutenant can't answer back, so I'll say no more." Without delay Wade began to formulate schemes. He had in his youthful days been partial to the literature of adventure, and to romances, and he tried to reconcile his imaginative efforts with the facts of their position. At the outset he proposed that together they should dig a tunnel, beginning at the floor of their dwelling; explaining that he knew where a skewer was to be had, and that French prisoners of war had built ships out of bones with no better tools. Mark repudiated the suggestion :,-withont discussing it. Undismayed, his companion spoke of bribery: an idea which was allowed to drop when it was discovered that the pair could not raise ten shillings between them. Such of their treasures as they had not lost had been stolen from them, a fleecing concerning which4Wade had remarked that it was enough to make a man thankful that he was poor. "If we only had a balloon now, with water and grub, and the wind was fair, there 'd be no difficulty," said Wade, regretfully. "But we haven't. There's nothing for it but trusting to luck. You've a lot in your favour. You're only a limper, so to speak; they don't watch you very sharply now, simply because it never enters their heads that you could possibly do more than hobble. Seeing that you're bent on going, I should wait for a dark night, then clear out—l know a trick that'll get you out of the sleeping-place—and make for the point where that drowsy, dark-bearded sentry is on duty. He seems to sleep half his time. If you can catch him unawares well, it'll be his own look-out. You can't stick at trifles. You know the chap I mean? .Boota, they call him." "What's the name ?" asked Mark, quickly, recollecting vividly all the kindness which the placid woman at the farm had shewn him. "Boota," answered Wade. "He's a farmer chap—his father's one of the burgher guard, too. I have heard it said that they're not so very keen on the business, and that they'd be only too glad to chuck the thing up and go and look after their property. They were commandeered, and sort of sent on at the muzzle of the rifle. They're not considered trustworthy enough to take the field; so they're stuck here, whereon eye can be kept on them. I've heard, too, that the wife of the old man— who's the young man's mother—is a sort of Englishwoman. Their farm's somewhere near that God-forsaken place where we were herded for the night after we were caught. Well, remember what I've told you," concluded Wade. "Be guided by me, and you can't go far wrong." "I'll not forget," Benn promised. "I'll make sure of the chap in the daytime; then I shall know him. I have a feeling, somehow, that he'll be useful." The days passed, but not so wearily now that Bann was pledged to his escape. From time to time Wade startled him with projects for effecting his deliverance; but the schemes were so wild that Mark rejected them instantly. With all his useless projects, however, Wade was a valuable ally. In his own fashion he was as deeply interested in the escape as Bonn, and incessantly watched for opportunities to help him. His face beamed with delight when one day, a week after they had talked the matter of the escape over, he joined Benn, and whispered that he had a priceless treasure for him. 'With this he produced a tiny compass, a little gold charm which Mark had sometimes noticed on a chain worn by Wade. "I never thought of it till just now," he said. "When I was bagged, I took it that my watch and chain would go, so I just twisted the compass off, because I valued it, and shoved it into this hole." held up his jacket, and shewed Mark a small 'hole, big enough to push a sixpence in, between two folds of cloth. "I thought the compass might be useful. Then I forgot all about it till just now. when I happened to feel the thing. Lucky, isn't it?" "It's a God-send," replied Mark. By George, if I only had Prince !" He sighed, and Wade looked sympathetically at him. "Yon may ome across Prince, you know. By the way, old Tally-ho will have him by this time. ,You know he wanted him. Wouldn't it be a huge 'joke if you could turn up at the battalion and politely say to the old chap that if he'd quite finished with Prince you'd be glad to have him back ? " "That's where I shall make for, first thing, all , being well," said Mark. "As far as I can tell, ,our people are about as near to Pretoria as any- body else. It would be a bitter thing if they were first in, and we were out of it." "As for that," said Wade, with a laugh, "aren't we in before 'em ? Well, take care of your treasure—l've kept the skewer for you, and I think I can lay hands on a penknife. You'll find 'em all ;useful. You see, you might easily pin a snake .with a skewer an' trim him with the- knife. It isn't pleasant sort of feeding, but a man's often ,been glad of worse. Then there are locusts, and there'll be lots of chances of slices from a horse. I shall expect any morning now to wake and find that you've gone." Mark had suddenly become more hopeful. ITe was beginning to believe that after all his desperate attempt might be successful, and that he would yet live to rejoin his battalion and Lizzie ; and perhaps even to claim Prince from the Major in some such dramatic style as Wade had pictured. I But at the mention of the name of Boota, and the hints as to his unwillingness to take the field, a fresh idea had entered the trooper s mind. Would it not be possible to secure the disaffected burgher as an ally? No sooner had this thought come into Benn's mind than he set himself to learn all he could as 'to the sort of man that Boota was, and to try and calculate how much he might rely upon the burgher's help, if he should find the young man friendly. He found on consideration that Mrs. Boots had said Several things which could only mean that she and her family had no wish to take part in the war, that all they desired was to be left in peace, and that it had not been very willingly that the two men had left the farm and journeyed to the capital. If she had not put it into so many words, Mrs. Boots certainly had given Mark to understand that if he made known that he had seen her and that she was well disposed towards him, both the son and father, if they could, would make it in their way to serve him. : Wade's remarks, too, were of use. That shrewd trooper knew pretty nearly all the current:gossip of the place, and all the weak and strong points of the people who were set to guard them, and more than once ite had declared that if he had any thought of escaping, and needed a helper in the ranks of the enemy, that helper would assuredly be Boota, the burgher. Wade, on this point, had laboriously explained that it would be a case of humanity, and not bribery and corruption. "The man's heart's in the wrong place," lie said, "from the Boer's point of view. Ile's pro-British, if he's anything, and I believe would rather help a prisoner to get away than try to keep him. Bilk you see, they can afford to have one or two slack chaps like that, for what's to become of a man on the veld, even if he gets clear of this hole ? He can't live on dust and rocks—and, as far as I've seen, that's about all you can catch on the veld. No, they rem ln't bother their heads about the soft- looking bur:;aer ; he's good enough for his post. He's like the man whose heart was in the High- lands, chasing vlie deer, when his body was some- here else." Wade had rambled pleasantly concerning Boots, and Bonn, not knowing who the burgher was, and not then having definitely resolved to escape, had listened idly. Now, however, lie strove to recollect everything that he had heard and seen, and in the end was satisfied that he might set to work to learn how far he could depend on Boota as a helper in his scheme.

(To be continued.)

JOHN GARLICK-

More about his Hull Transactions. Further interesting details respecting the notoriouS John Garlick's Hull trading transactions were revealed at a meeting of creditors held at Hull on Tuesday...4l2e Official Receiver said that sixty proofs had been ten- dered, representing about £1,700. There was no state- ment of affairs, for the simple reason that the debtor had absconded. With regard to the assets, he bad had them valued, and they were estimated to realise over £1,200. There were very few book debts. When Brooks was arrested at Cardiff some money was found on him—over £2o,—a gold watch, a brass chain repro- sentiog gold, • gold seal, a rule, a knife, and other aiticles, In addition there was a £5 note, and in the house in Corporation-road, Cardiff, there were found three complete clothing outfits. The young woman who eloped with Brooks had returned to Hull with sorne money in her possession, two pairs of blankets, two carriage rugs, and a new hymn-book. (Laughter). This young woman when questioned declared that Brooks had only given her sufficient to pay her railway fare home from Cardiff. Subsequent investigations, however, brought to light the fact that she had invested £lO in the Savings Bank, and had bought herself several articles, including an umbrella, for which she gave a guinea, a travelling tag, and other things. The money had since been appropriated to the estate. As son as che petition was filed appli- cation was made for the appointment of a special maaa- ger. Having regard t) the great difficulty with tba workpeople, among whom there was nearly a riot, and the threat to wreck the premises, it was very desirable that a special manager should be appointed. Mr Wilfred Smailes was therefore appointed, and bad done wonders in the short space of time he had been in that:position. He had discovered assets, which be bad turned into gold, which otherwise would have been difficult to get at. Mr H P Horsley: There is a rumour that Brooks is an undischarged bankrupt from Liverpool. The Official Receiver: It so, I am sorry for the creditors.

Mr Wilfred &tulles, accountant, was appointed trustee to the estate, and five gentlemen, representing the principal creditors, were appointed a committee of inspection. Mr Horsley rematked that the Governor of the Hull Gaol, who had been at Staßord when Brooks was serv- ing 20 months there, had said that Brooks was the Cleverest criminal that he had ever met,

CURIOSITY REWARDED.

Blackburn Girl's Great Success.

Curiosity is, to a certain extent, ingrained in every human being, but it is generally accepted that tha gentler sex possess more than their fair share. Be this as it may, Miss Elsie Winifred Carter is ready to admit that a little inquisitiveness on her part proved more valuable than she ever anticipated. This young lady is 19 years of age, and she lives with her mother and sister at 88 Bolton-road, Blackburn, being a dressmaker by occupation. In conversation with Miss Carter at her residence a reporter from the" Blackburn Standard and Express" : learnt of the aforementioned curiosity and the manner , in chich it was satisfied. " August has been an unlucky month for me for the - past two or three years," Miss Carter remarked, " for I have always broken down in health at that time, which is the end of the season. I used to come home at night from my work feeling very sickly and exhausted, and ' everyone commented on my pale appearance. This went on unchecked until I became completely run down. Though I took medicine it seemed to have no , effect whatever upon me."

At this point Mrs Carter, who was present, inter- posed and said, " Yes ;my daughter was really very ill, and many a morning I have heard her say, wearily. "Oh, I wish I was not going to work, I am so tired al- ready." In her spare moments she would fall asleep and yet wake up even more exbausteci, which was strange, seeing that she was fcnd 01 being out and about. But I will tell you how the trouble was conquered. In my shop (a chemist's) I sell Dr Williams' pink pills for pale people. 1 you know, and I had decided to take some myself. I opened a box and left it in a drawer in the shop. I subsequently noticed that my daughter seemed to be getting over her illness quite remarkably, and one daY when I went to use the bia of pills I found it empty. I made inquiries,and Elsie told me she bad taken them all. unknown to us. and she felt certain her improvement in health was due to Dr Williams' pink pills." " Naturally," continued Mrs Carter, "we were de- lighted with the result of my dear girl's inquisitiveness which led her to try the pills. She continued to take I them, with the result that she has quite recovered her t 0 00hneter cheeks."dhe health and strengtb,and the colour bas returned the l.ThreeEowmanyinterviewer, e replied Mies Carter, " and.! abionxeaslld,.,id you:take altogether P "asked feel like a different being, and I can now do my as a dressmaker without the old tired teeling,wbile the heavy depression has quite vaniehed and I feel well and deerful."

"And it is your conviction that your present state ef health is due to Dr Williams' pink pills for pale people?" " There can be no doubt about it," was the decisive reply. " And•vou are willing your experience shall be made public ? " _ .

" Oh, yes." replied Miss Carter, " Dr Williams' pink pills have cured me, and I am grateful." Mrs. Carter backed up this statement, and added that she would not fail to recommend them as much as sh e could. The reporter elicited the fact that Miss Carter'S father, who was a chemist at Barnber Bridge at tbe time of his death- 15 years ago, was formerly a dis- penser at St Bartholomew's Hospital, London, and that Mrs Carter was a nurse at the same institution.

THE TRIUMPH OF THE TRAM. ---- American Millionaires Buying

English Trams, It is found that towss a few miles apart can be con - fleeted together by the electric tram at less cost than ha railways. Having satisficd himself that " that's so,'' the American millionaire sees it coming, and forthwith proceeds to buy trams and build electric light railway - The great work of connecting up popular places iii England is almost a monopoly at present : only two or three companies are seriously pushing extensiocs and forming new electric tramwaN companies. The British Electric Traction Co., Limited, are doing well, and their shares are at a high price. The 'lmperial" shareS are changing hands at no less than £25 each. Once, only a few years ago, it is said they could be got for 7s Bd. What fortunes are sometimes trade! Now we are told that Mr Torn Nevins, the New York millionaire, is taking over the Electric Tramway Ccn- struction and Maintenance, Co., Limited, 34, Victoria - street, S.W. This is one of the oldest, having been formed seventeen years ago. Some well-known pro- moters are to be bought out, and the company is to pioneer a great tramway construction scheme in the North, which is to rival the Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway is public convenience and constant service. Mr Thomas Nevins, like Sir Thomas Lipton, another great Irishman, is popular in America, where be has laid thousands of miles of track, and still ranks as a railroad king. He owns forty miles of estates in Ireland, where he is retiring to finish his days, while he amuses lumseli in England by giving us new electric tramways in several towns. In Cheltenham he has built, and is CO running,several lines. What are the Electric Tram W 3 Construction shares worth now ? Are there any brglera or jobbers who know them? Mr Nevins's plans would interest a few nibblers in these parts, who are not blind to the great future of electric traction.—" Blackpool Times." •

After an attack of influenza, pleurisy supervened and I wasted away to a shadow, without the strength of a child. I had a racking cough and, my stomach rejected all food; then medical men stated that I had developed consumption, and after treatment in a hospital I was discharged as incurable. Finally I took Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People and improved immediately. After five boxes I resumed work, and could enjoy food. lam now actually increasing my weight."

Spoken by Mr. GEORGE HARVEY, Wood Cottage, West Bergholt, Essex, to an Essex Telegraph reporter.

Education Department calling attention to the defic- ency in the school accommodation for lementary pur- poses within the borough. Suggestions were made at the meetihir as to how the deficiency should be provided for, and finally a suggestion, whicn was a fterwards made into a resolution, was passed in the following form That the Town Council should apply for the powers of a School Board so as to enable the Council to supply such deficiencies." They all knew that the Government last year made certain suggestions in this direction, and they had reason to believe, that the powers now sought for would be conferred if this formal a.pplication were made. He need not go into any statistics. Phey had been given before, and all that could be said bad been said in order to show the urgency of the case and the necessity for action being taken, What was proposed would be much cheaper and easier of management than the alternative, a school board. He did not want to frighten anyone by dwelling upon the alternative, and he therefore would move the following resolutions That a Bill be promoted by the Town Council in the next session of Parliament to enable the Council to borrow and apply moneys for the Purposes of elementary education, to confer on the Council certain powers of a school board, and for other purposes," and "that the expense be defrayed in the first place out of the borough rate and ultimately out of money to be borrowed under the provisions of the said Bill." tiouccillor SHILLCOCK seconded. Councillor TINNION asked whether Alderman Shaw Green could tell them what would be the cost upon the rates of working this and alio what would be the prob- able cost of a school board ? He thought it desirable they should know the difference. He took it they would have practically all the powers of a school board at con- siderably less expense. Alderman MONKS asked the Town Clerk whether it would not be advisable to include in this Bill the neces- sary Provision for the completion of the [scheme which the Technical Instruction Committee had under con- sideration for secondary education. The TOW 3 CLERK : It is intended to apply for such provision. It is incluied in the resolution by the clause " and other Purposes." Alderman MONKS said his committee the other even- ing passed a resolution recommending the Council in case this Bill was gone on with to apply for the neces- sary powers and in that way obviate the necessity for a Local Government Board inquiry. Alelerman SUTTON observed that as so many of them had not had an opportunity of going into this question very fully, and in order to enable the Town Clerk and the Chairman to answer the questions waich had been put, he thought it would he desirable to adjourn the consideration of the question. The TOWN CLERK said thers was no time to be lost if the Bill was to be promoted in the next Session of Parlia- ment. Alderman SHAW GREEN said the matter would come again before the Council for consideration. Alderman SUTTON remarked that they ought to know a little more about it. The DEPUTY-MAYOR said the issue was an extremely simple one. It was either a School Board or this reso- lution. He did not think there was any necessity for adjournment. At the ineetiog referred to it was dis- tinctly stated that the correspondence between the Education Department and the town authorities was of such a character as to show distinctly that it was the intention of the Education Department to force a School Board upon thettown if certain requirements were not complied with and that speedily. They were all of opinion that if a School Board could be avoided by some means equally advantageous to the town and consider- ably less expensive it should be avoided—(hear, hear)— and it seemed to him that there was no question what- ever that the proper thing for the Council to do was to adopt this resolution, because if they did not get the powers of a School Board immediately they had reason to believe that their action supported as it would be by people high in authority would probably lead to the Government itself bringing in a Bill to make common the powers of a School Board and confer them upoo municipalities like themselves. In that case the cost of the administration of the powers of a School Board by the Council would be 50 per cent. lees than the cost of administration under a School Board. They would gain in economy and equal in efficiency a School Board. (Hear. hear.) He thought it was:their duty to piss the resolution. Councillor BENNETT expressed himself very strongly in favour of the proposal. The advantage in the matter I of economy, he said, was not the only important con- sideration. It was undesirable to multiply orstanizations, and: both elementary and secondary education ought to be in the hands of the same authority. A Town' Council also could be trusted to administer the affairs of education more broadly and impartially than a School Board, and would be less liable to be influenced by out- side considerations. Alderman SHAW GREEN said the passing of this resolu- tion was a formal matter. The question would have to I come before the Council in detail, and then all particu- lars as far as they could be obtained would be given. Alderman SUTTON : That's all right. The MAYOR (to Councillor Tinnion) : Is that satis- factory ? COURCHIOT TINNION ! It will have to be. (Laughter.) The resolution was then put and carried unanimously. • "Colour Sense."

At the Co-operative Hall, on ruesday,Prolessor W Stirling delivered the fifth of the course of lectures which the Combo Trustees have granted to War— rington on "The Senses : Sight, Hearing and Touch," "Colour Sense " was the subject under discussion. The lecturer pointed out that the im- pression on the retina was not quite evanescent, but could be measured. The inapres4on outlasted the stimulus. Dealing withthe subject of colour the lecturer asked, Had it any use and what use had it in the human body and in nature.? It was said that the all-considerate Almighty had made the colours for their pleasure, but he (the lecturer)was perfectly certain He had done nothing of the kind. Colour was certainly not a mere question of the resthetin art. Many colours were inherent and due to the mechanical and chemical constitution of the substance. There were colours which had no significance in nature, and there were plenty of other colours which were extremely significant. They would see, therefore, that the question of colour was to a large extent a utilitarian question' and the physiological side was the way to approach it. Shape was perfectly distinct from colour. ISome artists had a quick eye for form, and their artistic sense for form was secure, but they failed in the colour. There was a colour sense. There were certain human beings who were unable to detect colour. Colour in nature, was due to a vibration of the ether. When it vibrated slowly they saw the colour which was called red, when it vibrated a little quicker they saw violet, quicker still yellow, and quicker still green, and then blue and indigo. There was no such thing as objective colour. There were no colours in the rainbow, and when they sa-vi it, whatever it might betoken to them, it was merely a natural analysis of the white light coming from the sun, in which its different rays were spread out in the form of an artificial spectrum. In describing the retina, the lecturer informed his hearers that it was a portion of the brain. Pictures were thrown on the screen showing the beauty of its construction. Some interesting experiments in colours were shown in which some colours were retained and others reflected. An interesting lecture concluded with an experiment in light, demonstrating the ease , with which the audience could be made colour • blind. • W. T. WILLIAMS.—SPECIALIST in MILLINERY. THE NEW MAYOR.

Unanimously Elected by the Town

Council.

The time-honoured ceremony of electing the Chief Magistrate of the Borough for the ensuing twelve months, took place at noon on Saturday. The space in the Chamber allotted to the'pablic use was somewhat uncomfortably crowdea by towns- men interested in the proceedings. A number of ladies also put in an appearance and they were accommodated with seats near the Mayoral chair. Amongst those present were Mrs Henry Roberts and Miss Roberts, and Mrs J C Parr and the Misses Parr. All the members of the Council were present, with the exception of Councillors Brough, Burton, Duncan and Wilkinson. Thera was also a large attendance of officials. When the entrance of the retiring Mayor was announced by the appearance of the mace and sword bearers the assembly rose. At the request of the Mayor all present con- tinued st anding,whilst he announced that as it was the birthday ot the King, he thought it was only fitting that they should express their loyalty and sentiments by wishing him very "many happy re- turns of the day," and health to enjoy the years to come. He asked them to join with him in giving three cheers for the King. The suggestion was heartily responded to and three ringing cheers re- sounded throughout the Council Chamber. The 11favonlhaving formally presented the return of the councillors elected on the Ist November, the Council proceeded to the chief busine,s of the day and that was the election of Mayor. The resolution was proposed by the Nlayor and was couched in the following terms "That Joseph Charlton Parr, Esq., be, and he is hereby elected Mayor for the ensuing year, and until the acceptance of office by his suc- cessor." In moving the resolution the MAYOR said it was not necessary for him to say one single word to recommend the proposal to the universal accept- ' ance of the Council. They had already discussed and settled the matter, and although their business that morning was of a somewhat formal cha actor they were none the less hearty in putting the resolution and in asking Mr Pares acceptance of that very honourable and responsible position. They were quite certain of this, that although Mr Parr was not a member of their body, still his long association with the town, and his interest in all its varied matters, had made his name to them quite as familiar as that of anyone in the distrin They knew also that everything that appertained to the good ot the people would have his cordial support and his active interest. (Applause.) They also felt certain that he would be very loyally, ably. and courteously assisted in the work by Mrs Parr ; and as far as he (the speaker) was conernel he was sure that the members of the Council,with himself, would do all they could to assist Mr Parr during the year to carry out the responsible duties of the position, to teke as much of the burden of the work that should fall ordinarily upon his shoulaers upon their own, and to give him every consideration, seeing that for some time to come at any rate he would be somewhat of a stranger to the duties of the office. (Applause.) He begged to Imove the proposition. He called upon the "Father of the Council" to second the resolution. Alderman SUTTON remarked that after what had been said it might be considered unnecessary and superfluous to dilate upon the merits of Mr Parr or his fitness to occupy the position of mayor. Mr Parr was well known and respected not only in Warrington, but throughout the U ited Kingdom, and any attempt on his part to evtol his good qualities might be looked upon as presumptuous. He would, therefore, content himself by saying that Mr Parr conferred an honour upon the borough by agreeing to accept the office. (Hear, hear.) As they all knew, it was not an unusaal thing for a gentleman in a high position of life to be elected mayor of a borough: it had been done in several cases lately, and it was to be hoped that the inter- est which Mr Parr was about to take in municipal affairs would induce large and influential ratepay- ers to come before the constituents. Notwithstand- ing all the hard things which had been said against the present Council, he maintained that the town had never had a better lot of representatives— (laughter)—thau themselves. (Renewed laughter.l If these gentlemen thought they could do the work better, all he had to say was "Let them come and do it. Let them instead of grumbling so much, come into the Council and teach us how to do better." (Applause.) He, for one, should be very much disposed to be taught and receive a lesson. He had only to say in corroboration of what hal been said, that on his own behalf, and on behalf of the other members, they would do their best to assist Mr Parr and to make his year of office as happy, as agreeable. and as pleasant as it was possible to be. (Applause.) The resolution was then put to the meeting and heartily agreed to. The retiring Mayor and Alderman Sutton and the Town Clerk then left the room, and shortly afterwards returned to the Council Chamber accom- panied by Mr Parr wearing the chain and robes of

office. Having signed the usual declaration, the newly- elected Mayor, who had a cordial reception, said: In thanking sof; for the honour jast conferred the greatest you have in your power to confer, and a greater than I could ever have hoped to receive at your hands, I can only say that while keenly ap- preciating the compliaent, I am deeply sensible of the responsibility which must necessarily accom- pany it, a reaponsibiliy I dared not have under- taken had it not been for the assurance given me in the requisition in which you asked me to accept the nomination, that all the Council would do their utmost to assist me in the performance of the duties of the office. (Hear, hear). My connection with Warrington has been along one, and a happy one, and I may now say a very proud one, for it is a distinguished honour for one outside the muni- cipal Council to be chosen Mayor of an important county borough, such as Warrington, a prosperous and increasing town, embracing so many trades, in- dustries and manufactures, in wbAt, I think, we may, without undue pride consider one of the lead- , ing counties, if not the leading county of England, ' and it is said that" What Lancashire thinks to-day England will think to-morrow." (Applause). You have had many distinguished men as Mayors, since the incorporation of the borough. First and fore- most, our good old friend who has passed away, Mr Beamont ; and lam sure I may say that the last occupant of the mayoral chair, my predecessor, has by his enterprise, his industry, and his indomitable energy, earned the appreciation of not only the Warrington of the pre- sent, but also of the Warrington of future genera- tions„ by the public works, improvements, and embellishments inaugurated under his auspices. Of his work in the Council, of course, I can have no experience, but from my personal knowledge, I can speak of his courtesy and consideration for those, who like myself, are outside the municipal area, but take an interest in the town and its pro- ceedings. In addition to all this, he has ever been ready to lend a helping hand to any charitable institution or any association having tor its object the advancement, the welfare, the education, or the social improvement of the town, being ever ready to place at their disposal the accommodation of the Town Hall, and to give in most cases his per- sonal interest and assistance, by presiding at their meetings For myself I can only say that I hope to follow the good example of my predecessor in all the useful work he has done, and the kindly encouragement he has given to the various good works carried on during his mayoralty. (Applause.) I cannot promise you the same business capacity.

FRIDAY'S OPENING CEREMONY. THE REV JOHN YONGE AND RAFFLING AT BAZAARS.

The sale was re-opened on Friday evening by the Rev John Yonge in the presence of a good attendance. In opening the proceedings the Rev E T Reed referred to the completion by the Rev J Youge of the 25th year of his pastorate. He said that such an event was one that ought not to be passed without notice and in the name of the congregation and in this own name he congratulated Mr Yonge very heartily. Mr Reedlwent on to mention the fact that in a copy of the" Reformed Church Record," a journal formerly published in connection with the Reformed Church, he saw an account of the opening services of Emmanuel Church in March,lBB3, and in that account a reference was made to a sermon preached by Mr Yonge, in con- nection with those services.

The Rev Joni.; YONDE, in opening the sale, said that they in the Free Churches honoured Mr Re:•cl and the congregation at Emmanuel Church for the stand they had taken for Evangelical truth against the sacerdotal teaching which was prevalent In a great many of the churches around them. Continuing, the rev gentleman expressed his pleasure that they always seemed to have steered clear of any questionable methods of carrying on the sales of work in connection with that church. Personally he should like to express a word of gratification that the Vicar of Sr. James should insert in the papers so manly a protest against the detestable habit of raffling at bazaars. He (the speaker) entirely endorsed every word that Mr Powell said in that letter and he hoped it would have a very good influence for good in the management of future bazaars in Warrington. It was a very abominable thing that people who were trying to work for the cause of righteousness, truth and goodness in the town should allow themselves to be led out into such very doubtful and questionable paths as those to which he had made reference. If they could keep their sales of work entirely free from those things he did not think they should lose anythingeven pecuniarily, and they should gain something morally. They could not expect the Cause of religion to flourish if they attempted to promote it by questionable and doubtful means. (Applause.) In thanking Mr. Yonge for his presence, the Rev. E. T. REED said he was very glad to endorse all that Mr. Yonge had said about the desirability of keeping out those questionable things from bat tars and sales of work. It gave him pleasure to see the letter in the press from the Vicar of Sr. James'. He thought if they all took a stand of that kind these practices would soon be entirely removed. It was announced that the proceeds for the two previous days amounted to a little over £76.

TOTAL PROCEEDS.

The proceeds for -t-te three days totalled £95 14s. 93.

If so, you should know the cause and get a rem&ly. The commonest cause of that wretched, " always cold" feeling is what is known as "liver chill." Good blood means that you are able to stand cold. Poor, weak blood, that you always feel chilly. The blood is often made poor and weak by imperfect liver action. How? In this way. The liver extracts from the blood the ele- ments from which it makes bile. When the liver becomes weakened or sluggish—which often results from the heat of summer—it ceases to extract these substances from the blood. Result- the bile constituents flow all through the circulatory system, the blood is poisoned, it no longer keeps you warm. Your hands and feet become cold, you shiver, feel those miserable depressed feelings, have continual fits of "the blues," and become alto- gether most irritable and "out of sorts." So much for the cause. How about the cure? First put the liver into proper order, and keep it so. Giro it a little help at first until it gets going again. Chas. Forde's Bile Beans for itiliousness do this exactly. They are a liver specific. They correct the liver and help it to do its work. In disordered systems the bile is by their help extracted, the blood purified, and that natural warmth generated which ends for good those chilly sensations. Remember also that Bile Beans are purely vegetable, and that doctors now prescribe them. You may get them from any Chemist for Is. 1 fd. or 2s. 9d. per box: but be aware of preparations represented as being Bile Beans, but which are sold loose in pennyworths.
