Ambulance Competition at Warrington. The competition for the Crosfield Challenge Shield was held at the Warrington Gymnasium on Saturday, the following teams competing : O. ldham, Brierfield, Kendal and Blackpool. The judges were Lieutenant-Colonel C 3 Trimble, M.D., of Preston (the deputy commissioner), Dr Thomp- son, of Chester (district chief surgeon), Mr A L Garnett, of Burnley (district superintending secre- tary), and Mr Whittaker, of Accrington (district superintendent). The competition was very keen Oldham and Kendal tying the first time. They then had to undergo a second examination, which resulted in a win for Kendal.— At the close of the competition colonel Trimble in presenting the Shield to the winning team, said that the work done had been far above the aver- age.—The visitors then, together with the men ot the Warrington corps, adjourned to the Patten Arms, where a good repast had been provided by Messrs Crosfield. A capital entertainment fol- lowed, presided over by Mr Arthur H Crcsfield, and some goad songs were rendered. After the usual i toasts had been given, Colonel Trimble referred n glowing terms to the good work done by the St. John Ambulance men in South Africa. He stated that over 2,000 men had gone out to assist the Royal Army Medical Corps,B3o of whom came from Lanca- shire known as the No 4 District. Mr Arthur Cros- field also spoke of the work done by the men. and of the manner in which they ho.d responded to their country's call. He felt proud to preside over such a gathering. Mr Geo Crosfield spoke of the work which he had seen the men doing in South Africa, and although under very trying conditions, yet he said they did their work cheerfully and well, as was proved by the excellent testimonials brought home by the men from both civil and military doctors under whom they served.i Alder- man Roberts (deputy Mayor) looked n during the evening and received a very 'hearty welcome. As the time drew near for the visiting teams to catch theii trains the gathering broke up after singing the National Anthem.

EMMANUEL REFORMED CHURCH, WARRINGTON.

Annual Sale of Work. The annual three days' sale of work in connection with the Emmanuel (deformed Episcopal) Church, Warring 'on, was held last week. Wednesday was the opening day of the sale and despite the in- clement weather there was a numerous attendance of members of the congregation and friends to witness the opening ceremony in the schoolroom over Arp- ley Station. Texts and mottoes adorned the walls and the stails had been artistically draped. Alto gother there were six well arranged st ails containing a multitudinous variety of articles destined for sale as well as a tea room. In a circular issued on behalf of the congregation it was stated that the debt on the church building referred to in last year's appeal was, by a special effort, cleared off by the close of last year. It was also a matter for deep thankfulness that their missionary student, at the end of his third year at college, was awarded the first honour certificate Of his year. They trusted that at the close of his college course,' hey would have the jay of recording that he had taken his place by the side of other honoured workers in the foreign mission field. The following is a list of stalls and stallholders : Mrs Reed's s;a11, Mrs E T Reed, Mr and Mrs G Norman, Mr and Mrs Matthews; cmgregational stall, Miss Mason, Mr and Mrs William Atherton, Mr and Mrs William Dickens, Mrs Arrnstong. Miss Edith Broadhurst ; Christian Endeavour and children's stall, Misses Annie Booth, Ebel Fledge, Jeaks, Beatrice Carriage, Elinde, Nellie B yardman, Nellie Matthe vs. Annetta Mee and Lizzie Heald, Mr J Hodge and Mr H T Kane; fin aver and trait stall, alrs Albert Smithson, Mies Annie Harding, Miss Esie Taylor, Mr James Callan and Mr Arthur Matthews ; Sun lay school stall, Mrs A Gandy, Mrs 13 übErt Landon, Mrs John Lloyd, Mrs Frank Lloyd, Mrs J Bibby, Miss Clara Lloyd and Miss Alice Miner& ; refreshment stall, Miss Less, and Miss Eva Lees, Misses Allen, Straw, Elsie and Mary Sandy ; tea room. Mrs Henry Gandy, Mrs Bellwood, Mrs Eccles, Mrs Myles and Mrs Banner. Amongst those present at the opening ceremony were Mrs Armitage, the Mayoress (Mrs El Roberts), Mrs John Yonge, Mrs R Garnett, Mrs Clay, Rev E T and Mrs Reed, Rev C Finch, Rev T Bishop, Mr .1 Pecimore and others. After prayer had been offered by the Rev C Fixers, The Rev E T REED, in a few introductory remarks, said he regretted to have to announce the absence of Mr Arinitage, who was expected to open the sale. Mr. Armitage wrote from Newton Bank, Preston Brook, as follows :—Dear Mr Reed, I aim glad that you have got two strings to your bow, and that coasequentle my un- avoidable absence from your sale of work will hardly be felt. You will, I feel sure, believe that I regret I am unable to keep my engag,ment, and would not have! stayed away bet for imperative reasons. I am, in truth, afraid to face the fog, and for a few days have had to wait at home until it cleared away. To-day it declines to clear and I must remain in the house. Mr. Reed proceeded to refer to the interest which Mr. Armitage took in the Emmanuel Church and alluded to the fact that one of the memorial stones of the church was laid by Mr Armitage on October 9th, 1882. His (Mr Reed's) experience of Mr Armitage's kindness did not date so far back as that year, for his ministry at War- rington had not yet reached a period of ten years. At Christmas he completed ten years' ministry at Emman- uel Church and from the very outset of his work in Warrineton he had exoerienced the greatest kindness and sympathy fr im Mr and Mrs Armitage. In the milt of many difficulties and in connection with what bad been a very uphill fight there had always been a kindly sympathy extended to him and Mrs Reed. Referring to the foundation of the Reformed Episcopal Church in 1873 he rated that it was the outcome of the Tractarian movement in this sense that the Tree- tarian movement in this country and also on the other Iside of the Atlantic lei many Evangelical church people to see that it was hopeless to contend against the aacer- dotalism and ritualism within the Church of England in this country and t e Protestant Episcopal Church in America. A number of sincere, whole-hearted Chris- tian men and ministers decided in view of the falling away from the old Evangelical principles on the part of those within the Church of England and the Protestant Episcopal Church of America, that it was necessary to commence a new movement on thorougeholnyscieevnacnegestaitcc: and protestant lines. Like all new movements they had had to contend with great difficulties on both sides of the Atlantic, bat they had endeavoured to per- severe maintaining their testimen? for "their work was progressing in Eagland,Canads, and the United States, and in connection With their missionary work p y work in India, he was honing that their young friend and missionary student, Joseph Callan,would be able to takee s Mrs ainitage to open the sale mp raer n i n e at lhl iant a work,up on on behalf of her husband. (Applause.) Mrs AIMITAGE said she must apologise for the absence of Mr Armitage who could not attend that afternoon without running a risk. She persuaded him to stay at , home and really it was her fault that he had not come. (Laughter). He had given her some words to read to them sad she would do so. They were as follows :—We are very glad to be counted amongst the number of the friends and well-wishers of Emmanuel Church to whom you look on occasions such as the present to take their turn in these helpful offices. You are to be congratulated noon the success which has attended your constant efforts to pay off the remains of debt which were incurred in the building of your church and although it has sometimes appeared to be very uphill work to those who have had the burden of responsi- bility to bear, we may say that those of us who took any share in the rearing of Emmanuel Church have nothing to regret. Church treasurers with whom I have Some acquaintance as a class are generally person- ally afraid of contracting debts which seem likely tn hang over them for years to come, and very properly so if the obligation is greater than can be overcome by , strenuous effort, but on the other hand, debt, and struggle and necessity are better stimulants to exertion and are more formative of strong cnaracter and self- reliance than is the condition of luxurious ease which ' comes when we have all that we require and want for nothiog. We are glad thus to help those who have proved themselves able and willing to help themselves and hope that your sale will realise all you anticipate." In conclusion Mrs. Armitage expressed the hope that the sale would he successful and declared it open. The Rev E T REED acknowle iged the services of Mrs Armitage and intinsated that be had received donations amounting to £23 73 61. (Applause.) Business was then commenced and the result of the day's takings, including the donations, was the raising of the satisfactory sum of £53 12s.

Mr Reed proceeded to refer to the necessity of Christian Unity and stated that he could not understand competition between evange- lical Christian churches. Co-operation ought to be their aim, for said he, they were all agreed on the essential points. Tiles were all agreed that the one foundation of the church was their Blessed Lard and Saviour,Jesus Christ. (Hear, hear.) They knew the difficulty under which those connected with Emmanuel Church were struggling. When they considered the number of people who never entered a place of worship, and whm he, as a medical man going from house t 3 house, saw the effects of the demon— drink, both he and Mrs. Harcourt were only too pleased to help such a cause. THE MAYOR AND CORPORATION AT CHURCH.

Sermon by the Rector.

:Drink, Gambling and Abuse of God's Day. The new Mayor, accompanied by members of the Cor- poration, attended divine service at the Parish Church on Sunday morning. Fine weather favoured the event, and many turned out in the bright sunlight to witness the picturesque spectacle which attends civic Sunday. Promptly at ten o'clock, the procession started from the Town Hall to the music of the Volunteer Regimental Band. The Volunteer Bugle Band was also in attend- ance and played occasionally en route to the Parish Church. The Volunteers turned out in review order and presented a very smart appearance. Colonel Ridgway Was at the head of the battalion which is now over 1,000 strong, and includes a capable cyclist section and a promising mounted infantry company. The local corps of the St. John Ambulance Brigade followed, in charge of Captain Waring, and the large number in attendance ass evidence of the increasing popularity of this useful institution. Superintendent Tamer and his shining helmeted firemen followed, and they Were succeeded by a posse of police with the Chief Constable as marshal of the procession, in charge. The sword and mace bearers (Sergeants Bell and Dean) with their antique and picturesque costumes carried tae Corporation insignia with dienity. The Mayor, wearing his robes and chain of office, rode in his private carriage in company with the Deputy Mayor,the Borough mem- ber and the Town Clerk. Immediately behind walked Aldermen Bolton,Francomie,Shaw Green ,Heaton Hutchinson, Fairclougb, Monks, and Sutton ; and Councillors Baird, Shillcock, A J Jolley, Hulse, Davies, Pierpoint, John Evans, W Roberts, Peacock, A Jolley, Ellison, Rogers, John Roberts, Smethurst. Forshaw, Sampson, Malkin, Bennett,Tionion, and Wilkiason. Tbe officials followed as well as members of the Board of Guardians including Mrs Clay. Amongst the local townsmen in the procession was Mr A H Crosfield. On arrival at the Parish Church, the procession was met at the gates by the Rector, the assistant clergy and churchwardens. Seats bad been reserved in the centre of the church for those in the procession' and when the service began, the church was filled in every part. Tile lessons were read by the Rev. Morley Stevenson and the Rev EJ Willcocks and the prayers intoned by the Rev G W Dickinson, 8.A., and the Rev A F White,M.A. (assistant clergy). Mr FM Darby, Mus Bac.,presided at the organ with marked ability and the singing was exceptionally well led by the choir. The sermon was preached by the Hector from the words Who is wise and he shall understand these things ? prudent, and he Isbell know them ? for the ways of the Lord are right, and the just shall walk in them ; but the transgressors shall fail therein." Hosea xiv. 9. He prefaced his address with a few words of welcome, and thankfulness, that his Worship the Mayor of War- ' rington and members of the Corporation and the other officers and representatives of the State were gathered together in Church that morning and for two reasons ; first because without God's help they could do nothing. They, as believers and as christians. trusted in Gods help. Secondly their preseoce was a sign of the con- tinuity between the Church and State. Might he remind them that for over 1600 years, from the time of Con- stantine, the Church and State had been united, and in this country for more than 1000 years had it been so. And long might it so remain. Might he further preface his address with a few words of recognition and:apprecia tion of the work done by the retiring Mayor and the retiring members of the Corporation during the past two years? It had been an anxious and difficult task during the sad war in South Africsaand they had reason indeed to feel grateful and thankful for all that had been done by the late Mayor. Proceeding, he asked to be allowed to speak a few words of one euragement and of anticipation for the future, and ask all present to pray that God would give grace and strength to the new Mayor that be might be able to act wisely, fearlessly, impartially and efficiently during his term of office. The Rector the,n proceeded to allude to three great national sins—drinking, gambling, and the abuse of God's day. He did not say drunkenness first,but drink- ing to cxe.cesa. The expenditure upon strong drink last year was £160,000,000-1 great increase upon the year before, and the population of the British Isles was about 40,000,000, which gave an expenditure to each petson, man, woman, and child, of £4 per head, and the ex- penditure ot each family would, therefore, be about £25 a year. That was the average. Suppose a workingman who has to feed, clothe, and educ-te his children spend- ing 103 a week ! Men drank much more than was good for them without ever being drunk. What could be done? Coull not the number of licences be decreased ? Could not the number of hours be shortened, especially on Saturday night and on Sunday ? He passed from drinking to gambling. It had been thrown in their teeth that in their Church bazaars and sales of work they encouraged the gam- bling spirit. Ho had tried to consider that very care- fully, seriously and earnestly, and he had always looked upon raffles as a most innocent thing, because he did not think that he and many others had the gambling spirit. Many persons had no desire whatever when they put in a shilling to win an article worth from 253 to 303, but he said to himself that it was possible that others who were putting in their shillings had a gam- bling spirit, and had desired to win. He had, therefore, come to the conclusion that perhaps the wisest course was not to have the raffles, although he believed them Ito be innocent in themselves. He had been told that little boys and girls selling newspapers had been seen at midnight gambling for half-jeeanies upon the pavement, and that parents saw no harm in their chil- dren gambling. Now, if it was true that it was the great curse of the country, and likely ta be a greater curse in the future even than drinking, they should try to do everything they could to stop it. Allusion was then made by the Rector to the abuse of God's day. Now it was timply a day of enjoyment, a day of rest for the body. The workingman said "I have been working six days, I shall lie in bed, I shall read my paper, I shall go tor a walk, I shall go by some excur- sion to the sea-side or into the country," and he never thought of all the trouble and work he was inflicting upon others. This was to be God's day, a day of rest for the body, a day of worship for the soul. People might enjoy themselves still as a whole if they did not inflict trouble and work upon others. In conclusion the Rector stated that one of the objects for which the offertory would be made that morning was the restora- tion of the Parish Church. Many of them loved the old Parish Church—the old Church of Eugland, the old Catholic Church of Jesus Christ. Many bad separated from it perhans in consequence of the fault of the Church, but God grant that they might come back again. Be was sure that in their hearts they did love that old church and would like to do something towards its restoration. There was a great deal yet to be done. At first £12,000 was suggested, but the scheme was cut down to £3,000 beeatue the matter of the schools was of urgent importance, and he did not like toopreasnsthies matter. They had about £1,700 of the £3,00, d h was sure that some of them, perhaps all, would like to eive something that day, that that house of God might be made still more beautiful, and still more worthy of the great God to whose service it was dedicated. The offertory having been taken the service concluded with the singing Of the National Anthem.

The King's Birthday at Warrington.

Saturday being the anniversary of the King's birth- day, flags floated from the principal commercial establishments and public buildings in the town. At the annual meeting of the Town Council on Saturday three hearty cheers were given for his Majesty at the request of the retiring Mayor, Alder- man Roberts. The Junior Conservative Club, Orford-lane, was brilliqntly illuminated with fairy lamps in the evening, and rockets were sent up at intervals in honour of the event.

Presentation of Royal Humane Society's Certificates.

An interesting little ceremony tock place at the con- elution of an inquest at the Warrington Police Station on Wednesday evening, when the Deputy-Coroner (Mr F A Jones) presented vellum certificates awarded by the Royal Humane Society to William Chumley (12), who resides in Edmund-street, and William Melling, who is employed at Messrs Houghton'a wireworks and lives at 12, Sutton-street. . . _

In making the presentation the Deputy-Coroner stated that the circumstances which resulted in the certificates being awardcd formed the subject of an inquest conducted by Mr Brighouse a short time ago. The deceased, a youth named George Evans, dived into the St Helens and Sankey Canal with toe object of Swimming to the other side but sank. The boy Chum- ley, who was only twelve years of age, thereupon got into the water and went to deceased's assistance. He canght bold of Evans, but as deceased was pulltog him underneath, he was forced to relinquish his hold. Shortly afterwards Melling came up, and without divesting himself of any clothing dived into the canal to find the body. The water at this particular point Was seven feet deep and at times was in a very filthy and foul condition. At the inquest the jury and the Coroner recommended that Caumley's and Melling,'s names should be submitted to the Royal Humane Society for recognition by them and the Coroner had received the honorary vellum of the Society for presentation to Chumley and Melling. Con- tinuing,the Deputy-coroner said he thought the value of the certificates was enhanced by the fact that they were signed by the new Prince of Wales and would probably be among the last signed by him as Duke of Cornwall and York. Addressing the recipieuts Mr Jones said it gave him very great pleasure indeed to hand the certifi- cates to them. He was sure it must be gratifying to them to know that they had done their duty as English- men, in doing all they could to try and save the life of a fellow creature. He knew of no greater act that any person could do than to risk his own life in attempting to save that of another person. Be was sure that as long as they lived they would feel they had done a noble act. It was what they expected all Eaglishmen to do, and the trait in the character which Euglishmen ad- mired was bravery. The Coroner handed the certificates to Chumley and Melling,and the Foreman of the Jury (Mr Jones) offered to frame them free of charge. A colleation was made ' and resulted in the sum of 7a Id being presented to Melling and 4s to Chumley. In returning thanks Melling said he thought that if he had been on the scene a minute sooner he would have managed to have rescued Evans.

HUMAN FAILURES.

Why is it some people never succeed in anything Want of ability, you say. Yes, sometimes; but we con tinually meet with meo, well endowed with talent,whn never achievP any success. Want of moral character ? Yes, oftener, tint not always ; for do we not every day find multitudes of folks, who, transgressing no law, yet fail to obtain even modest comfort ? Of course we do. How many never du to-Jay what they can put off till to-morrow ? Sometimes a young man debates with himself whetter he shali enter the Army or not until his coll.ge chum returns from South Afr ea wearing the Victoria Cross ; or ponders whether he shall propose to the girl of his fancy or not until We other fellow marries her. Such people are usually just too late to I acquire whatsver they desire. As they enter the station at one end their train steams swiftly out at the other. That one man should be industrious and another idle can be accounted for quite easily. Heredity and early training have much to do with it, but not all. Because of a sluggish liver, many a man has acquired a reputa- tion foe idlmess. At No. 25, MountOrd-street, Spark- hill. Birmingham, lives Mrs. Taylor, wife of a bicycle maker, formerly in the Army. As a wife and mother pf a family Mrs. Taylor has many duties, which must have been sorely trying to lisr during those spells of sickness from which she formerly suffered. "It wag in my head that I was u0u5117 attacked," said Mrs. Taylor on the 9:h of April, 1901, " and I seldom went a fortnight without being prostrated for two or three days at least. Biliousness and constipation were my greatest ills. Sick headache and dizzUrsss used to warn me of what I should have to suffer, yet I kept on my feet until the very room seemed to turn round ; then, when it became impossible for me to hold my head up, I had to lie on the sofa. For 48 hours or more I used to be like this, only arising from my couch to be dreadfully sick. I had no appetite, and rapi,:ly became extremely weak."

To look for energy in a person suffering as Mrs. Taylor used to suffer is like looking for common sense in an Anarchist. This is how Mrs. Taylor freed herself from her trouble. " Having read in the newspapers of the cures effected by Seigel's Syrup, I resolved to give it a trial. The effect was most unexpected. One bottle alone gave me more relief than all the other medicines I had previously tried, and before I had reached the fourth bottle the change was so wonderful I could hardly realise it. was quite a new being. Since then Seigel's Syrup has been our family medicine. My husband and childrcn all take it when threatened with Plness, and we have never yet found it wanting. We regard it as the most valuable medicine in the world, and are not ashamed to let the world know P." In our Carnegie Competition we have awarded One Guinea to the following Rogers,lol, Blackman- lane, Grosvenor Terrace, Leeds : Miss L Driscoll, Dun- manus West, Toomore Post Office, County Cork ; John Heath, Pantg:as, Garthbeibic, Liangadfan, Welshpool ; A Cheater, junr., 35, Georgesstrept, Loughborough. Further awards will be announced in the newspapers from week to week.

The Woolston Drowning Fatality. The remains of the late Mr George Davies (21), who resided at 49, Allen-street, and who was drowned last Sunday week in the Woolston Canal, near Bobby's Locks were interred in the Cemetery on Sunday afternoon,in the presence of a large eon- course of people. The officiating clergyman was the Rev L W Thomas, curate of St Paul's, who ad- dressed words of consolation to the bereaved mother and relatives. In the course of his sermon 'at the church in the evening the rev, gentleman alluded to the sad accident, mentioning the fact that in a short time Miss Sankey, the young lady who was with deceased when he fell into the water, would have become the deceased's wife. The sad event had come upon her without the slightest warning, and he was sure that both she • and the deceased's relatives had the sympathy of the congregation in the sad loss they had sustained

RUNCORN SONS OF TEMPERANCE.

The fortnightly meeting of the above Order was held in the Greenway-road School on Eaturday, under the presidency of Mr Thomas Eteesom. Thera was a good attendance of officers and members. Mr Edward Key, sen., who was formerly an active worker in the division, gave an interesting address in which he referred to the three years he had spent in Wales. He said that though Wales was more temperate than England there was much drunkenness to deplore. He mentioned cases which had come under his own observation, and con- demned the so-called moderation in drink. At the close of the address the following officers spoke on the subject. Messrs Thomas Cooke, Thomas J Holt, James Johnson, Thomas R Whyte, and Thomas Clarke. On the motion of Mr D Bisbrown, seconded by Mr Samuel Tomlinson, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mr Key, and he responded in suit- able terms.

IT is second nature to Tory Democracy. Mr WiNsroN CHURCHILL r to espouse Tory Democ- zacy. It has the sanction of hereditary descent t in his case, and that counts for much to the Con- servative mind. It is not surprising, therefore, to find that in forecasting the future of the Unionist party, as the hon. gentleman did at the Constitu- tional Club, he should ascribe to the democratic element in Toryism the position of the " pre- dominant partner" in what is still euphonious],; termed the Unionist alliance. There is, no doubt, ' a strong root of Toryism in the democracy that is illiterate and uncultured, and seeks only its °vn. It cannot rise to the altruism of the Liberal ' doctrine of the greatest good for the greatest num- ber; it strives only for the particular good it desires. Renee on occasion it is attracted to Toryism by a community of aims and sympathies. But it 18 a case of extremes meeting, and it may well be doubted if a lasting; alliance can be constructed out of elements whose interests and tendencies are . diametrically opposed, though sometimes they May meet and unite on an opportunist basis. Mr. ' Cutatounr. meant, really, something of the same sort when he said that the provin .1e of Liberalism Was the promulgation of abstract ideas, and the Province of Conservatism was the practical appli- cation of these principles to the circumstances of , daily life. If this description of the situation were correct there would be no dispute—or very little dispute—between Liberals and Tories. It ' Would be simply a difference of opinion as to the . opportune moment for granting certain practical advantages to the mass of people. No doubt that is what many well-meaning people , understand by their Conservatism—or Unionism, as they now prefer to call it. But history is i Proving every day that the positionis quite other- w " Ise—that there is a sharply-defir.ed line of • demarcation which separates both the aims and ' methods t of Conservatives from those which are essentially Liberal. The underlying principle of government by the aristocratic and , moneyed classes ; its constant aim is the repres- sion of the Power of the people. The principle of 1 Liberalism is government by the people for the . people. When, therefore, Conservatives say they are a party of practice and not of theory, it , simply means that their theory, or guiding principle—for it is the, same thing—not being , popular with their friends the Tory democrats, has been waived—put aside for the time being k. —as the price of power which may be given or , withheld as the democratic element is appeased or . opposed. Such a party, of course, is in danger of losing its principles altogether ; and as it can't be for ever borrowing its opponents' ideas and mas- querading in stolen clothes, it is led into peculiar i difficulties. It is refreshing to come across the , ' simplicity with which Mr. CHURCHILL shows that . this is the difficulty his party experiences with _ regard to educail-o-n—. It was time, he said," to formulate definite Conservative principles on , questions like education." So that principles— , therefore theory—are necessary after all ; but When Conservative principles are formulated on , ~ education and like questions, such as housing, temperance, and old-age pensions, will they be in , accord with democratic ideas? That is the &fa- t culty which Tory democrats find Aver facing them. lir a osroep btuhtr o. tw ne ltdoetmh el ede do eg ti ne atbyeccraenatinee a. dfeesriireer be-for r lug allowed to share the same table.

THE ordinary public as a Parliamentary Pro rule care little for the cedure. discussion of procedure

in the House of Commons ; it may be good enough tor discussion among politicians, it is thought,but it is too intricate and tiresome in its details for " the man in the street" to take the trouble of Tasking its acquaintance. It is a pity this indiffer-

"1

ence exists, because without a genuine interest on the part of the electorate Parliament will not take the trouble to reform very much its own proced- ure. Many people must be puzzled to understand why bills come up time after time for discussion, and after lengthy debates are not heard of again till another session, when the question is again debated with more or less result. So the farce goes on, and many subjects have an almost annual outing in the House, only to leave off where they began. The fact is that, in spite of all the reforms which the country has seen since the first great Reform Bill of 1832, the House of Commons in its internal affsirs is one of the most conservative institutions in the country, and some of its old and so-called time-honoured customs are upheld Ely none more stoutly than the Radical front beach men and others who, sin- gularly enough, glory in" the traditions of the House" as if they were far beyond the touch of the profane hand of reason or common sense. There is, perhaps, some excuse f®r this, because the law and practice of the House of Commons are, in a sense, unwritten, and yet are of legal and moral obligation as complete and binding as any statute law of the realm. The fabric of precedent, indeed, is regarded as a fetish, and like all fetishes, it frequently stands in the way of improvements that are necessary to progress. Much discussion is now being given by statesmen and politicians to the subject, chiefly on the ground that, although the House of Commons sits on an average some- thing like nine hours a day, it cannot make a respectable show :of business done. Sometimes this is put down ,to obstructive talk; but if the true reason were really probed to its core, it,would probably be found that it exists in the fact that the methods :of the H 01193 are antiquated and obsolete in almost every particular. Take the ' mode of dividing the House on a vote. It is well- known that each division takes fifteen minutes, in which:every member has to leave the House, walk through one of the lobbies, get his name marked off, and return to his seat in the chamber. This is a tedious process, and yet divisions are repeated and multiplied in an inexplicable manner not only on the action of the wilful obstructive, but by reason of the rules of the House. Sir HENRY FOWLER, the other day, informed his hearers at Wolverhampton that in a ses- sion of one hundred and eighteen days few than thirteen days of time had been occupied by members in walking through four hundred and eighty-two divisions. Surely some of that time might be saved for the public service. The form of voting might be altered and simplified, and the occasions for voting reduced. The repeated formal appearances of Bills in the house during their pro- ceedare might be abolished, and business placed on something like the level it occupies in other places of business. That is probably what business men in the constituencies would demand if they took any interest in Parliamentary proceedure. In the meantime the few who can justly claim that designation in the House of Commons, and who are aware of the abuses that exist, might do something towards placing the business of the House on a more effective working basis.

Runcorn is in the throes of a School Board election, which is being fought with the utmost keenness on both sides. The "sides" are, of course, those representing church and state versus pro- gress and educational freedom. There is little of the latter in voluntary schools and not much more in Board schools when the governing body contains a majority of churchmen as has been the case at Runcorn for the past three years. The Church party obtained control at Runcorn on the strength of the cry "Down with the rates," which is just about on a par with the discredit- able piece of electioneering that won the last general election for the Government. The cry then : "the war is over" was only true in one respect. It was all over South Africa and it is yet. The same at Runcorn! "Down with the rates" has not saved anything for the rate- payers and they will be twice foolish men if they allow themselves to be again misled by the specious arguments of Canon Wood and his associates. They will get their interests far better conserved and their children far better educated under the regime of the Progressives, for whom we advise eery ratepayer in Runcorn to vote on the day of the poll.

----- The death of the Dowager Lady Carew removes from the list of British centenarians another of its most famous names. Only a few days ago the death of Mrs. Hanbury at the age of 107 was re- corded. Lady Carew had not quite reached those years; but at 103,:and before the rapid failing of her powers within the last few weeks, she poss- essed health and spirits rather suggestive of the autumn than of the winter of life. She was in no ordinary sense a link with the past. She was born before Bonaparte had even been declared Consul, and she was within a few miles of the field of Waterloo when the great Emperor was finally overthrown. She was, indeed, one of those pres- ent at the famous ball at Brussels on the eve of the battle immortalised by Byron. On 1/.0 reign of Louis Philippe she had vivid memorie;—as is only to have been expected,seeing that then,in the bloom of her Irish beauty,she was one of toe most charming of the ladies of the French Court. Her sprightly wit more than one chronicler notes; and she was famous also, it appears, for the wonderful 'gowns of Irish poplin with which she set a new fashion among the Court ladies. At Waterford she was highly popular, and Irish men and women of all ranks were proud of this great lady, with her white locks and her winning manners. She was a tie with the age of Wellington, and even of Nel- son, Of which there now remain but two or three in the whole kingdom. idenl

The report of the Chief Labour corresponu. A of the Board of Trade for 1900 shows that trade- unionism suffered very little check last year, not- withstanding the admitted failures of several strikes and other events adverse to the movement. The membership of all trades unions is returned as 1,905,116, an increase of 10.1,217 on the pre- vious year. This works out as an advance of 5•8 per cent., and on a comparison with other years shows that the rate of increase was somewhat slower than in 1899, but quicker than in 1898, a res.ilt mainly due to the growth of the coal-min- ing unions. Women now make up about 6:12- per cent. of the total, but their membership is almost entirely confined to the cotton trades. In the number of unions there is a slight falling off, oc- casioned mainly by the dissolution of some of the smaller bodies and the transfer of their members to the larger ; but only fourteen societies made a formal transfer of membership. It is to be re- gretted, indeed, in the interests both of capital and labour, that this movement for the unification of the unions in the respective trades is not more pronounced than at present. It is admitted by everybody that a great deal of friction is caused in the industrial world by the multiplication of unions with a membership of a few hundreds only. It is these bodies from which rash action usually ?tarts. The great unions have a sense of responsibility, and have as their officers men who, if at times they find it impossible to control the passions of the crowd, are generally gifted with foresight and almost statesmanlike ability. The arrest at Cardiff of John Garlick who escaped from Stafford Gaol in February, 1899, has aroused fresh interest in that notorious personage. It was in the early part of 1896 that he paid War- rington a visit and opened" business" in his own. particular style at Lythgoes-lane under the alias

Local tradesmen have good cause to remember his short but all too long stay in the town, for a number of them shortly afterwards became conscious that they had been" had" by a ' swindler of the most vicious kind. At the subse- quent bankruptcy proceedings he was brought 1 out of Walton Gaol to give evidence and he unblushingly unfolded a career of crime which was as black as it could be. Since his escape from Stafford Gaol where he was serving a term of imprisonment for obtaining money by false pretences, he has been victimising the good people of Hull in a manner similar to that exper- ienced by tradesmen in Warrington. He has given abundant evidence of his vitality and ingenuity since he cleverly escaped trom prison and those who have come across him will be prepared to admit that he is a man of "brains." He is, in fact, a striking example of a man's ability being applied in a wrong direction and his countrymen have reason to be glad that he is again in the charge,—and it is to beihoped securely—of a prison warder.

MATTERS OF MOMENT. -- A Provincial Letter.

By the Birthday Gazette of last Friday it did not appear that the list ot honours conferred was un- duly long, as has sometimes been the case, and names will no doubt occur to the mind of the reader of persons which he will feel ought to have been there. But their turn has, perhaps, yet to come. , "Taffy" at least must have considered himself a happy man, on the ninth, since by far the most important of the King's birthday honours was that which announced that the Duke of Cornwall and York takes the historic titles of Prince of Wales and Earl of Chester Welshmen in particular are glad that the title made familiar to them, and to the nation at large, by the present king is revived, although it has the effect of rendering out-of-date the Prayer-books that were new but a few months since. The brand new Prince of Wales has already show by the public spirit which marked his splen- did and successful tour round the Empire that he realises the spirit of the noble motto of his new , title, " Ich dien." According to Sir H Nicolas, six Royal Princes have been born Duke of Cornwall who were never created Prince of Wales, a title which dates from 1281. In the present age, how- ever, which has become so habituated to speak of the Heir-apparent as the Prince of Wales, the ab- sence of that title from the Royal list had been missed more keenly than many have, perhaps sup- posed.

--- Temperance Sunday does not appear to have been so generally observed in the provinces as is desir- able, from the temperance point of view. Sunday being the next day to that of the Mayoral elections is scarcely a sufficient excuse, inasmuch as it would apply with equal force to London, where it is said that the day was observed as Temperance Sunday in many of the churches, sermons being especially devoted to discussing the subject in its various bearings upon religious and civic life. There is at least as much and as good reason why Temperance Sunday should be generally observed in the pro- vinces as in:the metropolis. Two years ago a deter- mined "crusade " was being carried on in all parts of the country in favour of legislation against the sale of intoxicating drink to children. It was pointedrout that additional legislation was not needed, that the law, as it stood, if carried into effect, would do all tbat was required. The agita- tion. fashion-like, changed, and war has since been waged against juvenile smoking. but while the de- mand for cigarettes proceeds by leaps and bounds, that movement, too, shows indications of flagging. Temperance Sunday efforts to stamp out the sale of intoxicants to children, and to suppress juvenile smoking have all been fashionable, and like the fashions in regard to dress or the Blue Ribbon Army, appear to have their day.and then disappear before the advance of another all-absorbing "whim on the part of excitement-mongers, to be again scarcely seen or heard of

People accustomed to think of the probability of what they read being true are scarcely surprised to find that the reported "drugging and robbery epi- sode 'on a south coast railway has been followed by a confession. Yet the story will have been of some , benefit it it helps to impress upon the public mind the wisdom of the adoptten of the Corridor princi- ple. In a corridor carriage, except it was occupied only by the victim and his assailant, the safe com- mission of a crime would be almost impossible. The railway carriage criminol abominates the corridor just as the burglar hates the light. That the corridor carriage will yet be generally adopted,even on slow and local trains, is probably but a question of time. The passing of the old rolling st3ck will, on the majority of railways, define the time-limit.

--- While, in Yorkshire and in parts of South Wales, towns have been suffering from a decided scarcity of water, and local authorities are obliged to limit the supply, the tarmers, by way of change, have ceased to grumble and are actually congratulating each other on the splendid autumn they have enjoyed, right up to the lap of winter. The fields, they say, have been "dry," and worthy of cultiva- tion ; the cattle are tit and ready to clear up the remnants of the autumnal grass crop without tread- ing the soil in the least. Thus, what is loss to manufacturing and " business " towns, proves to be the gain of agricultural districts and the farmers. English dairy produce is stated to be very slow, from the trader's point of view. notwithstanding the short " make"; and'the reason, it is added, is not far to seek. It is put down to the imports.

There are few towns in the provinces that have not suffered from the Revolver Nuisance and the presence of the man who, although he has no hesi- tation in pointing a rifle or pistol at another, is in- variably innocent of any evil intent because enabled by the present state of the law to plead that he " didn't know it was loaded," or " only did it in ' fun," when he sees the consequences of his folly. The statement recently made by the Birkenhead coroner on the subject is good, as far as it goes, but does not go far enough. All that the Excise authori- ties do, apparently acting under legal advice, is to see to the collection*of revpnue, and leave the "carrying of arms" consideration to the police, or anybody whom it may concern. What is needed,is an alteration of the law so as te place authority in in the hands of the police, and to make the "point- ing "of fire-arms at others a crime, Otherwise the peril, and the "nuisance." must be perpetuated,and go on for ever.

Seldom has the old ssying "it never rains but it pours," received more ample and striking verifica- tion than at Blackburn, one of the towns which had been affected by the water famine, where on Tues- day the inhabitants were made to suffer severely by the downfall and presence of too much water. the river overflowing its banks, and the centre of the town being flooded to the depth of four feet. The gale was not, of course, equinoctial, but the forcible expression of an independent cyclonic de- pression of unusual area. 2or Ireland suffered as severely as Scotland: and the English provinces as both. One peculiarity of the sharp visitation was that the wind in certain districts was accompanied by rain, and in others by snow. Snow fell notably in Westmorland and the Midlands, and in Scotland, while there was a tempest of rain in Ireland.

JOSEPH WOODWARD, BUILDER AND CONTRACTOR, BEWSEY-STREBT, WARRINGTON Repairs to Property punctually:attended to.

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD ELECTION.

Meetings Next, Week. As will be seen from our advertisement columns a meeting in support of the Ratepayers candidates will be held on Wednesday next week. The progressive candi- dates will hold a meeting on Monday evening in Green- way-road School at which Mr J Wilson will preside. We also refer our readers te the election addresses of the Rev J Chambers and the Progressive party.

" Progressive " Meeting. On Friday evening at the Brunswick School the Pro- gressive party held a further meeting in suppott of the canditure of Messrs S Taylor, W Handley, F J Norman, J Pritchard and II H Posnett.—Mr S Taylor occupied the chair and there were present on the platform—the Revs G E Cheeseman, Alfred Wells, W A• Eyre, J Osborne, M.A., and Messrs F J Norman, J.P., R H Posnett, J.P., A. It Norman, J.P., J R Frith, J Wilson,W Handley, and J M Frith. The Chairman said he was sorry to announce that Mr. Griffiths, the principal speaker, could not be with them owing to illness. Mr. Handley said it was a difficult thing to appear as an apologist. He had been with Mr. Griffiths on the Monday previous and he was then looking forward with pleasant anticipation to coming to Runcorn. He could, however, promise them that Mr. Griffiths would be with them before the 27:11November. (epplause.) Mr Handley then read a letter to the effect that Mr Griffiths was detained by illness. He, Mr Handley, was pleased to say that they had a gentleman, Mr Cheeseman, Hinton, Liverpool, who was a strong educationalist,and who woul I be able to enlighten them on the education question. Toe Chairman who was received with applause, said that some three years ago they ran five candidates on progressive lines. Unfortunately they were beaten on the cry of " Down with the rates." It was utterly without foundation to tell them tney were going to lower the rates. They knew perfectly well that the rates had not gone down. He had no doubt they would now have some other cry. They would have some good trump card to play. Last night they had two hours of it and from the discussion they were quite able to judge what plank it was going to be. When the Church party got into power some three years ago they turned Mr Pritthard out of the chair which he had occupied for 12 years. They gave him marching orders and put in his place Canon Wood and made Mr. Imison vice-chairman. The Pro- gressive party were rendered powerless and they had practically no voting power and therefore they had to grin and boar it. It was a most humiliating position. He would far rather let then) have the whole nine mem- bers than the five. They might protest but they were quite powerless as far as voting went. When they can- vassed the people last election the people said that the side who were to lower the rates was the one to vote for. For the last three years the Voluntaryist party had had the sole control and keeping in view the cry of down with the rates, he would read them the monies which had been got from the rates for the three years previous to their management. In 1896, 1897, 1698, when Mr. Pritchard was in the chair the money they had had from the rates amounted to £4,950 and for the last three years it had amounted to £6,150. Down with the rates ! (Laughter and applause). They could not keep the rates down and they knew it. The Rev A Wells: It was:the is strategy of truth." (Laughter). The Chairman : Anything you like, these are the facts. They might cheat an Englishman once, but they could not do it a second time. It would no deubt be put before them in a form that would almost make them believe their cry. They would probably say "We I will turn our sehoola on your hands now, and then where will your rate be." He told them that if they did so it would be one of the best things that had happened for a long time. The ratepayers should go to the Beard Schools and look at them, and then go to the Denomi- national schools. The children from 4 to 14 years of age had to spend the best part of the life in school, and they should spend it in schools which were thoroughly efficient and sanitary. If they were to turn the schools over to the Board he challenged whether the rates would go higher. tie would go further than that and give a guarantee that if they got the same grants fee the schools as Board schools that for three years the rate- payers should not suffer. At the Halton-road school they paid £3O for the use of it as a Sunday school, and In such an event they (the clerical party) would have to pay their share. Had they looked at the back of their Poor Rate ? For the first five half years it was , 41d, but in this the sixth half s ear it was 4 I. Was that down with the rates? Rev A Wells : ft is the Irishman's rise. (Laughter and applause.) During the last year said the Chairman the Voluntary School bad received £3,356 from taxation every penny of which the working man helped to pay. The working man had no control over that. Was that just, or right or was it fair play ? He did not object to them having it PO long as they were doing good service with it, but ' he strongly objected to denominational school managers taking possession of the schools of the ratepayers. It was the reverse of business t) put them in charge of their own schools and the Board as well. Ere did not think the ratepayers would put them in power again. (Applause.) Mr J R Frith said he was pleased there was going to be fight and hoped that the voluntary party would be turned out of power. The Rev G E Uheesman, who was received with loud applause, said that he understood there was a battle rpyal on Thursday night at the Runcorn Board meet- ing. One of the members, he had heard, was a Canon and the result was that the Cseon was spiked. (Laugh- ter). " The strategy of truth " was a favourite maxiM of Canon Woo' a and in spite of whatever might be asserted the true reason why the clerical party wanted to dominate the Board was the better to enable them to keep their own schools alive for religious propagandism. The conscience Clause was practically powerless aid to send children to schools where they were taught doc- trines contrary to those of their fathers was an outrage. In the 14,000 national schools in England only church- men need apply for situations as teachers. A School Board, when not dominated by the priest, never asked any question relating to their religious convictions. A candidate as a teacher was judged purely on his merits as an educationalist.Taxation and representation should go together and if they were hOping to maintain Canon Wood they had far more right to control his schools than he had to control the ratepayers. Perhaps his nostrume)f the "strategy of truth " to confound the people would be trotted out again. But the expenditure on education was one oflthe most useful expenditures for it equipped them to fight in the great industrial war. The School Board taught the Bible—the greatest of moral books—and gave their employees the best salary. The reason why the Voluntary Schools were worked on cheaper lines was because the teachers were sweated. (Applause and shame.) He hoped the Pro- gressives wsuld win the day: The Rev Alfred Wells said the other day he had the Christian courage to preach a sermon on "Nonconform- ity not Schism." If necessary he would preach it again and again, so firm were his convictions on the religious and education question. He had no objection to sectar- ian schools provided they would pay their own way. But he refused to be taxed to keep up day schools where doctrines were being taught to children contrary to the religious beliefs of their parents. They (the Progressives) would not go in for the "strategy of truth " doctrine. Let the other side bring their charges. They were bringing them against him (the speaker), but that was a light matter. (Laughter and applause.) He hated alie and he had a profound contempt for the lie that was half the truth, for it was aver the blackest of lies. The rev, gentleman then spoke about certain correspondence which had passed between him and the Rev Mr Stables, Weston, or certain church children which attended the Wesleyan day schools. If he could not get a decided answer he would publish the whole of the correspond- ence in the press. He had been accused of being a bigot but this was absolutely untrue. He had given the church children permission to a ttend the recent mission at Weston, but he could not let the whole of the school go. He trusted that between eight in the morning and eight at night they would be at the palling booth and vote for the progressives. Mr R. B. Posnett, one of the candidates said he was in the unfortunate position of being the 10th candidate. He was only a little one and he hoped to slip in with the nine. (Laughter). He felt they must do their best for the education of their children. The rich and the peer should have the same chance. He stood before them to represent the best interests of the ratepayers. He would have nothing to do with any party cliques but would do the best for education that he possibly could. Mr F. J. Norman then stood up and received quite an ovation of applause. The Chairman: I should Jike to:say the hero of the day. (Applause). 11A. Voice: And the vicar is not here to shut him up (Applause). Mr Norman, when the noise had subsided, then said the hour was too late (cries of go on ! go on) to say anything. Producing a bundle of Volnntatyist election papers dated three years ago, Mr Norman said they acted on one gentleman he knew like a red rag to a bull. He would not have his name printed on staff like that for all the wealth there was in Runcorn. He had had bis innings last night (Thursday) and it was a pretty good one. He wished they had been there. They knew that on School Board matters he was one of the most Peaceable men in the world but it was fated that he should be one who should struggle to resist the evil in- fluence that was corrupting their educational system. The Runcorn Board at present was far different than when Dr Gastaldi and Dr McDougall were members. Those two gentlemen always put education first. If the churchman would put education first and creed after- wards he was quite prepared to retire. He would have other opportunities of addressing them at length and he hoped that the rate Payers would support them. A vote of thanks was afterwards passed to the Rev G

E Cheeseman.- The meeting wss most enthingastic and there was a very large audience.

Meeting of the Catholic Interest.

.. On Wednesday evening at the Catholic Schools Mill Brow a meeting was held in favour of the candidature of the Rev Fattier Chambers. There was a fair attend-

acce. Mr Imison was in the chair and he was supported by the candidate and the Rev Father McGrath, Warring- ton. The Chairman said that tha Catholics had the right to claim the same privilege that other people enjoyed. It was unreasonable that Nonconformists should be able to receive the religious education they desired for their children and that Catholics and Church people should not enjoy the same privilege. (Applause). The Rev Father Chambers bad been a thorough loyal supporter of the interests of toe ratepayers. The one thing which affected the ratepayers at the present time especially was the expense that education entailed. He would not depreciate the value of education but they should not overlook the cost of it. Workingmen at the present time had a difficulty in making both ends meet cense- coently economy should be practised. It was univers- ally admitted that Voluntary Schools were cheaper than Board Schools. Everybody felt the burden of the poor rate. Father Chambers was one of those who had done his best to secure efficiency and economy. They had done nothing to reduce the usefulness of the Board Schools but had dose everything required for education without unnecessary waste. Father Chambers said he asked to be returned in justice to the Catholics of the town. Could another gentleman have been found he would gladly have re- tired but the Catholic priest was looked up to in edu- cational matters. Education in order to be thorough must be accompanied with religion. (Applause.) The good formation of a child's character was ,necessary to make him a good citizen. This could not be formed with mere secular knowledge. There was, of course, dogmatic teaching but religious knowledge was given with it. Every school under the Government had given with it a certain capitation fee called" Tee grant " which was estimated according to the results. Then there was the fee grant. This was given because there was no charge for teaching. Then came the crux of the difficulty. The Board demanded from all the people alike the payment of rates towards the Board School. The Board came forward and said they wanted so many hundreds ot pounds per year to work the Board Schools. The Voluntary schools neturally asked was this fair ? The present Government then allowed them the Aid Grant, but was it to be compared to the other, on the one hand they got as much as they liked and on the other about half as much from Government as they applied for. The aid grant did not come from the ratepayers as taxes, but from an old whiskey tax that was levied on whiskey t 3 which no doubt their ancestors paid. The money was in the hands of the Government and they applied it to educational purposes. The voluntary contributions with his school totalled to £55 103, and because there was not sufficient to meet the expenses of the schools there was what was called an overdrawn balance. That year he had had to raise between £5O and £6O. The Pariah Church had an overdrawn balance of £4s2;Newtown,L33 Trinity £5OO. If these sums had to come out of the rate- payers it meant increasing rates for Runcorn. Rather than give their schools up to the management of the Bard they would keep hera for the sake of religious education. It had been urged that the parson wanted to look after his own interest. At Weston they had an overdrawn balance of £lOO and, though he did not wish to boast, the greater part of that amount afterwards: came out of his own pocket. He asked for their support. The Rev _Father McGrata afterwards addressed the audience. Votes of thinks closed the meeting.

Meeting of the Church Candidates.

On Tuesday evening in the National Schools Runcorn, a meeting on behalf of the candidature of Messrs Cunn- ingham, Icnison and Littler and the Rev Canon Wood was held, Mr W Timmins presided. He was supported by the candidates and the Rev Wl3 Stables. There was a large audience. The Chairman expressed the wish that the ratepayers would put the voluntaryist candidates at the top of the poll as they had dons three years ago. The Rev Canon Wood, who was received with loud applause said he felt like a martyr because he was one of the best abused men in town. During the whole of his ministerial career he had never had so many hard things said about him and he really did not deserve them. He would not deal with the qiestion in a trivial manner for it was one to be studied seriously. At present he would deal with the manifesto which bad been issued in the Wesleyan Magazine. He would have replied to "the appeal " in the " Parish Magazine " but as it would not he issued until after the election it would be too late. What he was saying was intended for Wesleyans only, not as an attack but a reply.Doubtlese many Wesleyans would agree with him. The address in the Magszcne was to the effect that the primary object of Methodist poliey in the matter of elementary education was the establishment of Board S:cl000ls everywhere which was of special importance in rural districts like Runcorn where Wesleyan people had no alternative to the compulsory attendance of their children at Anglican Schools. Runcorn was not a rural district. There were 1,118 children in Board Schools in the town, and he was astonished to learn in Runcorn that Methodist children were compelled to go to the Church school, They were nct compelled to send their children to the Church school. (Applause). The next paragraph read that a School Board dominated by the clerical party was not an ideal state of affairs. In reply be would say that it went without saying that a echo,' dominated by the Progressive Party was not an ideal state of affairs. It was no argument, merely assertion. The next para- graph read that by clericalism was not meant the Church, but something entirely different. That was equivalent to suggesting that there was some difference between their clergy and themselves. That could only have been published with the intent to do mis- chief. The address went on to assert that , clericalism was the fanaticism of a priestly caste thrusting itself into a temporal domain. What was the temporal domain ? He should think the School Board. (Laughter.) Did it mean paying rates. He never thrust himself in that—(lsughter)—nor any one else cared to be toruat in that " temporal domain." To call them " priestly casts " was merely throwing mud. "It attempss to determine by spiritual preroga- tive questions in the civil sphere." Did that mean he WI s attempting to make them elect him on the School Board by some spiritual power. (Laughter). The magazine also said "It recognized in the education ot the children a ready means of acquiring power, hence the desperate attempts it is making to control educa- tion." This was not true, They did it because they thought it was the boat means of doing good. He pro- tested against the constant imputing of bad motives to people. (Applause). Whatever they did or said Re always asserted that they were not doing or spoaking that which was true. Such sagge3tions stung and were not forgotten for years and yoars after. He trusted that everybody would keep their tongues in restraint and not say hard things. He thought the town was very well settled in school until it became enlarged, and he hoped it soon would b increased commerce. They might need more in the futare but not at present. They would give their strongest opposition to erecting more Schools because it could only be done by increased expense. In the bill mentioned in the magazine article the schools would have been managed by the priests and churchwardens. Yet this " magnificent project that was wrecked in the House of Lords by the Bilhopa "was the very thing they, the Progressives wanted. The clergy would have bad full control and the hie was thrown out by the House of Lords. He (the Vicar) did not know whether anybody there had evor proselytiied from the Wesley- ans hut most of them could remember cases in which the Wesleyans had proselytised from them. The article further stated that " Our Mr Perks, MP. last year called the attention of the Education Department to the declaration which required the signature of a school mistress stating that she intended finally to separate herself from the Wesleyan body and return to the Church of England." Be (the speaker) had written to the Secretary of the National Society about the matter and read the reply which stated that he (the secretary) bad never seen or heard of ' such a document. (Applause.) Mr. Posnett had stated that he (the Vicar) was having his son taught by a clerical tutor. Mr Samuel Taylor sent his son to be educated at Alvanley Vicarage. He was not abusing Mr Taylor ;he Mr Taylor) was a very wise man. He (the Vicar) had devoted most of his life to education and for anyone to assert that they did know what they were talking about was ridiculous. He then quoted from one of the local papers a speech delivered 3rd December 1898. "It was an old saying that there was no strategy which confounded peeple so much as the strategy of truth. Their party printed and issued the truth, and throughout had said what they meant. They took for their motto ' keep up the Voluntary Schools and keep down the rates.' " That was what he said and rlthat was preciseley what he meant. The words "strategy of truth " were being re- ported all over the town by mischievous tonguee. Every child in the town should be able to rise from the bottom to the top of the ladder. The Progressive candidates were not the largest ratepayers as had been asserted. The Manchester Ship Canal paid £435 for the last half year, Land N W Railway Company, £177 M A Company, £l2O ; Haan Council, £5O ; Gas Company, £lO2 ; Co- 2m2eir : atimver TSaoyelioert,y,32ll.93s; 40der hies; t0n5,1213 Nornaan,22 6s 8 I;Mr J Pritchard,in conjunction with his brother,22 Ils ;Mr W Handley with Mr Rhodes, £1 93 ; and he the (Vicar) paid £3 13s 33 and beat them all. UMr Cunningham said he told them plainly he meant to support the voluntary Schools. The rate three years ago was 93 and if the Progressive had aired their fancy ideas it would have exceeded 9d. The Church party said "down with the rates "and as the ratepayers appreciated them three years ago they would doubtless do so again: They had religioue liberty in Runcorn and believed in the workingman's child having the best of everything. Votes of thanks closed the meeting,

INTERESTING PRESENTATION AT WESTON. On Sunday afternoon at the Men's Bible Class held at Weston Church, the Rev W II Stables at the request of the Royal Humane Society presented Mr Ernest Morefield with a certificate for his splendid conduct in attempting to rescue from drowning the unfortunate man named Samuel Rawlins, Widnes, whn was drowned in the Weaver Canal in July last. DEATH OF AN OLD HALTON RESIDENT. One of the oldest residents of Halton has just passed away in the person of Mr George We'shy, who resided at Halton-road. The deceased, who had been employed at Messrs Ochleston's tannery for quite 60 years, retired on a pension about live years ago. He was well-known as a steady work- man and a faithful friend, and was held in high esteem both by the firm and his fellow-workmen. His death took place on Friday afternoon, after about three weeks' confinement to his bed, during which time he had received the unremitting atten- tion of his medical attendant, Dr. Robinson. He was a member of the William IV. Lodge, Manches- ter Unity of Oddfellows. RUNCORN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY. DIED IN BEM SLEEP. On Tuesday morning at the Runcorn Police Court, before Ar It Dobson, coroner, an inquest was held touching the death of a child named Margara Pendleton, 9, Queen-street, aged four months.-31r Cooke was the foreman of the jury.—Charles Pendleton stated that the deceased was his child On Sunday she went to bed with witness and his wife: She had had a slight cough for about four days.—At 1.30 his wife asked him to take the child and he found her quite cold. They at once went for some neighbours, afterwards fetching a policeman. The child was insured but the policy had lapsed.—Mary Pendleton, wife of the last witness corroborated. She had rubbed the child with camphorated oil for the cold.—Dr W Bethune stated that he had made a post mortert examination of the deceased who had died from pn-umonia.— A verdict in accordance with the evidence was returned.

WESTON WESLEYAN BOLE AND MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT CLASS. On Monday evening the weekly meeting of the above was held when Mr J H Spiby uf Widnes, read a paper entitled The makings of a man." Mr John Macleod occupied the chair. The essayist at the outset said it was not his intention to inform them as to how one could become rich or even how to invest money to advantage. His aim would be to show how it was poseible to become a good man. It was imperative upon all that moral force should be in the ascendency, Which combined with qualities such as the exercising of the faculties of observation would do much towards the attain- ment of success in life. Man was not measured by his stature but by his intellectual attainments. In order to succeed it was essential that young people should study in early manhood and thereby acquire,a store of knowledge which embraced such subjects as theology, biography. and science, the acquisition of which gave an impetus to effort by means of which together with adherent faculties tehuechyaosundg maneterm determination, fully equipped for the race and energy, of life and achieving of success. "Tis not in mortals to command success ; but we will do more, we will deserve it." He, the essayist, felt all should be in- spired with those words, even amid the incessant struggles and trials of life, and should " rise on the stepping stones of their dead selves to higher things.' Instances were quoted of England's great men who after repeated failures were eventually successful in attaining the highest pinnacle of fame. Disraeli's repartee while addressing the Rouse of Commons that a daywould come when he would be heard was indicative of a determination which in after years was fully demonstrated. The temperance question was one which was exercising the minds of all who had the welfare of their fellow creatures at heart. Much was hoped fram legislation, yet it was almost impossible to make men sober by Act of Parliament. What was needed was that the populace should be educated to higher ideals—the acquisition of which would enable man to avoid the pitfalls and shares of life.—Man's environment was entered upon at some length, the question evolving as to whether environment tended to lower the moral standard, or id there were a hidden source within which enabled man to overcome the degrad- ing influences which surrounded him.—An animated discussion followed, when A Cross, J Spruce, J Brown, W Hazleburst and others took part.

INTRODUCED

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD The Final Flourish.

Ethics of Electioneering. Three years ago the Sectarians captured the School Board, and during their period of control seldom has a ripple disturbed the serenity of their contentment. The last monthly meeting was held on Thursday, November 7th, and was quite a coutrast to the usual placidity of the proceedings, for the "waters were troubled"almost from the start. There was in fact a "full dress debate" arranged in " due and solemn " form as the Freemason woula say, upon the want of accuracy of Mr Imison, Chairman ot the Finance Committee, and upon the ethics of electioneering. Mr Pritchard was the only absentee member, and the expectations of the publio may be gauged by the fact that the members of the Press equalled the members of the Blatd, and there were in addition Mr Lightburn, the Clerk and his assist- ant, Mr &Reed Lightburn. The Board Room is not a large one, and it was not long en the atmosphere was sultry in more senes than one. The scope of the debate was shown on the agenda and Mr F J Norman opened the proceedings by requesting froth Mr Imison an explanation of a statement la.-! had made at a Board meeting in May ; namely that the precept then issued was £5O less than last year ; and consequently there was ground for congratulation on the part of the rate- payers. This statement had no foundation in fact ; the precepts for the past three years were exactly the same —£2,050 per annum—Mr Imison replied that what he said, or meant to say, was that the precept was £5O ' less than the previous half-year. There was cause for I congratulation in the fact that although the Board had done more work the rate was not increased.—Mr Nor- man retorted that the great cry of the Church party at the last election wan " down with the rates," and to say that they were getting more for their money would not satisfy the poor deluded people who had expected a reduction in the rates. He moved a resolution that the Board considered Mr, Imison'a explanation unsatisfac- tory and he set it forth in writing at the instigation of the Chairman, who then scrutinized it with the exacti- tude of a pedantic pedagogue, dotted the I's and crossed the T's, as it were. before it met the approval lot his scrupulosity. Mr. Handley formally seconded the motion , in order that it might be discussed, remarking that Mr. Imison bad practically withdrawn his statement. Mr. Imison de- nied this impeachment and added that he could point out many cases for congratulation. The Chairman trotted out the good old excuse that probably the mis- take had been made by a reporter, which drew from Mr Norman the observation that the reporters of the three local newspapers had all made the mistake. The Chair- man condemned making public business turn upon the veracity of individual members; it was a course which he hoped no one but Mr Norman would pursue because it could only lead to bitterness of feeling. He had heard outside that Mr Norman had suggested that the conduct of those gentle- men who did not agree with him on the Board was founded upon a want of truthfulnesi. Mr Norman ac- cepted the statement with a decisive " hear, hear " and the chairman then reminded his brother members that he was also vicar of the parish, at the same time ex- pressing his belief that Mr Norman was a religious man and addressed to him a " solemn appeal " that in the matter" which has to be fought out and will be fought out and has been fought out on honest lines," Mr Norman and those who thought with him would not embroil that town with feeling and cause bad blood that would last for years and years but give each other credit for honesty and for truth. Mr Norman replied that he was as strongly grounded in his convictions as the vicar was in his and what he complained of was that matters were put in such a way as to codvey a wrong impression to the outside people, His religious training had perhaps given him an idea of truth different from other peoples. It had been said in Runcorn •• There is an old saying that the strategy of truth is the best way to confound the pL,ople." He had nothing to do with strategy of truta ; he told the truth as straight as he could, and in many quarters he had spoken strongly with regard to a bundle of rubbieh that he carried about with him. Here Mr. Norman displayed a big bundle of election hand bills issued by the church party at the last election and he proceeded to challenge the Vicar t make good a statement that the taking over of the Wesleyan schools by the Beard had put an extra 41 in the £ on the rates It the Vicar could prove that assertion, Mr Norman undertook to withdraw all be had said with regard to , the election literature. If on the other hand Mr Norman pre,ved as he hoped to prove, that chat statement con- veyed a totally wrong impression to the people, he and the Vicar would understand one another better in the future.—The Chairman said there was nothing in that leaflet he did not maintain to-day.—The discussion on ' this subject was ended by vote. Only Mr Norman took the affirmative, four were against and three members did not vote.

Now followed the consideration rl what may appro- priately be called "eection ethics." At the last election, Mr. Norman explained that the Church party said the School Board rate was 9d in the £ ; the Progressives said it was 7i; and he propodnded this proposition. In the last year of Mr. Pritchard's chairmanship,£l,7oo was drawn from the rates; last year £2,050 was drawn from the rates. Mr. Imison had described the latter sum as eq.:a! to 411 in the £ for the half year ; what rate then did the former suet represent. Both Mr. Imison and the Chairman cleared up the mystery by stating that at the election the Church party were dealing with the current rate, a rate levied a week or two before the election which equalled 9.1. for the year. The two parties were dealing with totally different figures, and added the chairman it suited the 3 church party to deal with the current rate. Mr Norman made some scathing re- marks at this method of conducting business. To deal with prospective expenditure was altogether irregular. A. Beard ought to stand or fall by its previous three years' work. If the chairman of the Cc-operative Society at the next annual meeting of that body would put on one side the official balance sheet and begin to talk about something purely speculative of the future, no matter how remote, he would establish a reputation for eccentricity, to say the least of it. The chairman again assumed his lecturing tone to Mr Norman whose policy, he said, was to close up voluntary schools, and to show how this policy was at variance with the Wesleean body he mentioned that the Wesle,yans at Northwich were building a new school. Mr Norman rose to reply, but the Chair- man would not listen to him and curtly ordered him to sit down, and Mr Norman, perforce submitted, observ- ing that the Chairman always most unfairly used his I powers in the chair,. Mr Handley, however, rose to • "put the Canon right " on the Northwich matter. The Chairman's remarks, he said, might convey a wrong impression. The Wesley-Ins of Northwich wire building a new school, because through subsidences, the property on either side of the school had fallen away, the school had been shored up !several times and was positively dangerous. The Wesley ans were not building a new school as a matter of educational policy, but as a matter of necessity.—The Chairman : You don't contradict that they are building a new school P.—Mr Norman t No. but he contradicts your inference, the impression you would have created as to why the Wesleyans of Northwich were building a new school. It is the • strategy of truth again. t This closed the electioneering business of the Board

and the financial t itcrnent for the past year was pre— sented : and Mr Itnison pointed out that the balance in hand was £941 3.7 e 3L—Mr Norman said the ratvayers would not agreo with them in taking money to Parr's Bank and keeping it there. A perusal of the Voluntary School balance sheetq showed a very different policy. A precept of £l,OOO for the current half year was ordered to be served upon the overseers.
