UNHAPPY Boma LIFE. At the Warrington Police Court on Monday before the , Mayor and other magistrates. the adj,urned case was heard in which Elizabeth Hclbrock, 47, Watkin-street, was charged with neglecting her children.—Mr Browne,l who prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C.' said the defendant's husband had been a teetotaler tot 15 years. The cause of complaint against the woman was drink. She was before the magistrates on August 19th, and her husband thought then that if she were given a chance she w4)uld turn over a new leaf. She, herself, promised that she would take no more drink. lhe case was ad- journed for four weeks. During that time she made only one slip. A further adjournment was applied f 07, and granted, and that very same night she was drunk. A. :week afterwards also she was found drunk by a police officer. The time was midnight, and she had a baby with her. She was summoned, and fined for the offence. She was drunk again last week but one. For a week she had been ill and unable to leave the house. The chil- dren were not actually neglected, because tar Holbrook's mother lived at the house, and looked well after them. The motiier would have nothing to do with them, and was away from borne continualls--Mr Holbrook gave evidence and said that on one cceasion his wife came home late, blackguarded him and tore hia clothing. He gave her all his waves. At one time he began to pro- vide the food for himself, but she threw it out of the house and quarrelled with him for not giving her his 7r.eney. He had t' no life " with her, and desired to be separated from her.—Mr Browne said that Inspector Hackett had come to the conclusion that no good would be effected until the woman was sent to prison.—lt was suggested that she should be sent to a home.—Mr Taylor Police Court Missionary, promised to get her into one for six months, and after some persuasion she consented to go, but it was afterwards found that as she had a baby enly three months old she would not be admitted to the home. The magistrates decided to send her to prison in the second division for a month SCHOOLBOYs' COATS STOLEN FROM SANKEY BOARD SortoOn. WARRINGTON. Local Spiritualism. A public teaparty was held in the Spiritualist Hall, Bewsey-street, on Monday evening, the edibles for which were contributed by the ladies engaged in the promotion of a sale of work, After tea an entertainment was held. After a few introductory remarks hy the Chairman, Nliss Ethel Chapman, of Stretford, opened with a pianoforte solo, "Rondo in C major," for which she was encored, and in response played " Yorkshire Bells." Later on the same little lady gave a selection, " La Poupee," and was again encored, when she responded with Haydn's " Harmonious Blacksmith" with varia- tions, which was loudly applauded. Miss Chapman also accompanied several songs in capital style. Miss Edith Sherburn sang " Daddy" in such a manner as to secure an encore, and in response obliged with "The Cows are in the Clover." which was well received. Mr Sberburn gave a reading entitled " Editha's Burglar " in a telling manner, and was followed by Miss Furnees, wno sang "'a hen there's love at home," and later on she sanein an equally pleasing manner " Hearts and Homes." Miss Lily Gallop recited very effectively " The Indian with his dead child " (encored) and "The Orphan." Messrs Ciayton and Stout sang the familiar duet "Larboard Watch," and, being encored. " All's Well." Mr C Furness recited in his inimitable style " Owd Shunt," and had to respond to an enthusiastic encore. The whole programme was thoroughly enjoyed. Lantern Entertainment in St. Paul's Schools. On Monday evening a lantern entertainment was given in St. Paul's Schools, Kendrick-greet, by the Rev L W Thomas and Mr Robson J Chorley. A large portion of the pictures were from photographs taken last Walking Day Portraits and views of the Vicar and Mrs Tarbuck. St. Paul's and All Saints Sunday Schools, teachers and children in procession, lorries loaded with infants, and the various schools assembled in front of the Town Hall were thrown on the sheet. There were also pic'uree of the children at play and at tea in Bewsey Park. Teen followed a miscella- neous set,showing Sunday schoolteachers at Lien- gol'en, All Saints' choir at Rh),•l. and All Saints' football team playing a match,and soon, ending up with some very beautiful slides made by Mr Charles and Mr John Chorley, which elicited great applause from the assembled company. The entertainment was varied by songs and instrumental music given by the Misses Chesworth, Miss Nellie Armstrong and Mr Ormsby. The programme concluded with the singing of" God save the King." Presentation at Cockhedge Mission. At the close of Cockhedge Mission Sunday School, on Sunday afternoon, Mr Hall, superintendent, on behatf of the teachers and senior scholars, pre- sented to Mr Andrew Scriven a splendidly-bound teacher's Bible, bearing the following inscription : —" Presented to Mr Andrew Scriven, on the occa- sion of his leaving the town, as a token of respect, by his class and workers of Cockhedge Mission Sunday School, November 10th." Mr Hall alluded very feelingly to the loss the mission would sus- tain in parting with such an earnest and faithful worker as Mr Scriven had been during his stay amongst them. Mr Scriven, in a few well-chosen words, thanked Mr Hall and subscribers, remarking that the I:lrusal of the pages of the Bible would alwa3 a ren.und him of the many pleasant hours he I had spent in connection with Cockhedge Mission. Industrial Developments at Leigh. It is authoritatively announced that the Wigan Coal and Iron Company are expending over £20,000 on developing fresh seams at their West Leigh mines. The extensive anchor cable works, Leigh, are now completed and electrical cable manufactur- ing will shortly commence on a large scale. Fifteen thousand pounds have been expended on buildings and £20,000 on plant. It was originally intended to establish the works at Gorton, but the company have acquired eleven acres of land at Leigh,and ex pect within five years to erect indiarubber work-

IHE EXAMINER FRIDAY, NOVEMBER 15 1901

===== 0002601_19011115_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
ALDERMEN RE-ELECTED.

The Proceedings at Saturday's Council Meeting. Considerable interest centred in the first business transacted by the Warrington Town Council under the presidency of the newly-elected Mayor on Saturday, namely, the election ot aldermen. There were five aldermen whose terms of office expired: —Messrs Henry Roberts, W Bolton, R W Francomb, R Heaton, and T Hutchinson, and all sought re- election. A circular had been sent to each member of the Council by Mr E L Fleming, Secretary to the " No Municipal Trading "Committee which has re- cently been conducting an agitation against muni- cipal trading, direct or indirect by members of the Corporation, calling attention to the resolution passed at the meeting of the ratepayers held in the Co-operative Hall. The circular concluded as fol- lows :—" On behalf of the committee appointed at the first meeting! have now to call upon you to give effect to the wish of the ratepayers in regard to the re-election of Aldermen on the ninth inst." It was anticipated that several Councillors intended to make personal explanations at the Council meeting, and there was an unusually large attendance of the public. In the Council there were four absentees— Councillors Brough, Burton, Wilkinson and Duncan each of whom was re-elected on November Ist. The voting was by ballot, and the papers having been handed round. Councillor TINNION said that before they pro- ceeded with the election of aldermen he asked to be allowed to make an explanation. He bad received a circular from the "No Municipal Trading Committee " in which they dealt by resolution with what they termed serious charges against prominent members of the Corporation, and then they went on to ask that the whole of the mem- bers of the Corporation should vote against the retiring aldermen. He was present that day to give an explanation of why he should decline to do anything of the sort. (Hear, hear.) According to their own resolution they asked that the Local Government Board should make a thorough investi- gation into these charges, and then evidently doubt sprang up into their minds, and they wanted to know who were the responsible parties, if any. He &dined to do what he Considered an un-English thing, and that was to condemn a man before he had heard him. (Hear, hear.) He considered that if the members of the Council voted against the retiring aldermen, who were all eligible for re-election, they would do something that was unfair, because they would be asked to condemn them before they had had an opportunity of having that thorouah investiga- tion which he and every other member of the Council would th&roughly welcome. There was another matter be wanted to bring forward, and that was that on November 9:h, 1897 every then re.embrr of the Council pledged himself to the following document, and he thought it had been signed by every member of the Council who hadibeen elected 'since that day :—"We the undersigned members of the Warrington Town Council, hereby respectively agreelto observe the rule of seniority amongst the members of the Council in the election or re-election of the present or future aldermen of the borough." Under those circumstances he could not see how any body of men could expect them as honourable men, having signed the document, and knowing so we'l its contents to go against their word. He should con- sider that any member who voted otherwise in the cir- cumstances guilty of a gross breach of honour to his colleagues. Therefore. that was the reason he intended to vote for the re-election of the retiring aldermen. (Hear, hear.) Councilor BENNETT did not think the pledge just named by Councillor Tinnion should be binding under all circumstances. If he considered that his duty called upon him t vote against the aldermen he should do so. Alderman Surrox said it was not usual on these occa- sions to do more than make an explanation ; but he considevd that Councillor Bennett had made a speech. if everyme was to be allowed to make a speech there was no tAling when the discussion would end. It was quite contrary to the customs to make any speeches in reference to the election of aldermen and he thought th,y should refrain from doing so that morning. Councillor BENNETT said that it was not his intention to make a speech but:he did not consider that:the pledge was binding under all circumstances. It might be his duty to vote against it. It was however, un-English and unfair to condemn a man before he had been :tried and at present, he should vote for the re-election of the retiring alderman. The aldermen then retired and the Council voted. The papers having been handed to and examined by the Mayor he announced that every member whose paper had been handed in had voted for the re-election of the five aldermen. He declared them to be duly elected. Councillor Pierpoint was the only member who did not vote. The aldermen were then called into the room and informed of the result of the voting. They each read aloud and signed the usual declaration of acceptance of office. Alderman ROBERTS Ira returning thanks said :—On behalf of my colleagues and myself I have been asked te return our thanks to the Council for this expression . .

of their confidence. I think it is perhaps unnecessary for me to say that those who know us best have the utmost confidence in us, and tbe vote this morning is some reply at any rate to the unnecessary and scandal- ous charges which have been circulated during the past few weeks. (Hear, hear.) I personally thank the Council for this mark of their confidence, and express those same thanks on behalf of my colleagues. (Applause.)

PENICETH SCHOOL OLD BOYS V. PENICETH.

The retarn match between these teams was played on the village ground on Saturday. Youde, Dudley and Sinclair scored for the School and Bowyer for the village. Half- time, three to one. For the first quarter of an hoar from the resumption the home team swept everything before them, and before five minutes had elapsed had scored No. 2 goal. Bp_,..eouraged by this they again attacked, and a lovely centre bowyer was headed into goal, and the scores were equal. From a scrimmage in goal Dudley unwisely used his hands, and the usual penalty followed, placing the School a goal behind. The visitors kept up the pressure, and three minutes from time Youde with a brilliant shot that just went under the hitt' made the score equal again. They made energetic attempts to get the winning goal, but the defence prevailed and time arrival with the score still equal.

Lancashire League.

WARRINGTON AGAIN DEFEATED,

Warrington Visited St. Helens on Saturday and sustained another defeat. The Town kicked off with a slight breeze in their favour, and after alternating opening exchanges. Warrington were placed ol the defensive. A couple of corners fell to Warrington and Hoy saved well, enabling Leigh to get away on his own. He centred and Roberts scored.twhile M'Guffie shortly afterwards put on a second from a penalty, Leigh haying been fouled. Although so much in the rear Warrington were not dismayed, and they attacked vigorously, albeit their combination was defective and ttey were forced back, the Town nearly scoring from a corner. Aspinall saved an header from Roberts. The for- wards got possession, and found Wainwright and Stirrup in splendid form, and the Town left from a pretty pass by Houghton were only kept out at the expense of :a corner. Wild kicking was indulged inifand Lynes was beautifully robbed by Wainwright. Half-time—St. Helens Town two ; Warrlngton,lmil. During the second half the game was of an even description and no further goals were registered, the Warringtonians attacking occaslonally, hut their play in frank of goal being very wesk. Final—St. Helens Town, two; Warrington, nil.

Many boats have done much less than pay their expenses, and Several are being laid up. This is bad, both for owners and men, the latter seeing their hoped-for poundage vonirh with considerable concern.

THE NORTHERN UNION.

SOUTH WEST LANCASHiRE COM- PETITION. -- Warrington Win at St Helens.

Warringtoo were without a Northern League fixture on Saturday. but instead they played St Helen • at Knowsley- road in the South West Lancashire Competition. The day was fine and there was a crowd of some 2.003 people to witness the engagement between these old rivals. The home team were without Barnes while Warrington had to substi- tute Burgess and Duckworth at half-back for Allen and Hockenhall.whtle Ratcllffj tookiFista's place on the left wing Mr. 3 Priestley was the referee. Warrington lost the ton and started play against a strong breeze and immediately attacked. Harris got hold and run- ning down touch he punted across, where Isherwood fielded smartly and ran in behind the posts, scoring a sensational try. The scorer took the goal kick and hit the post with the ball, This roused the Saints who forced play to the other end Harris ran out to the centre, but the visitors were penalise.l Lre failing at goal. Warrington were pressed for some little time, but removed to centre. Carney here got away, and after passing by Thompson. Briers and Doherty, the latter got over, but was called back for a pass toward. Duckworth kicked out from a scrimmage on the line, and Foulkes took a free kick, hut again the two points were not forthcoming. From another free kick Foulkes kicked down, and the ball was scrummaged in front of goal. The visitors were kept busy defending, but Foulkes again failed at a free kick after several of his men bad had very hard lines in not crossing. The St. Helens forwards and half-backs were playing very strongly and Warrington had to give several minors. Duck- worth and Isherwood ran the ball splendidly to the St.Helens twenty-five, but keen forward work quickly returned Hallam got away on the other side, but St Helens were again back to the Warrington line, the wind giving them great assistance. Kennedy and Morris were unfortunate in being held on the line. b t in the next minute Hunter made the best use of a loose ball, and dodged over, scoring a try, from which Lee kicked a good goal and placed St Helens ahead. This success came very welcome to the homesters. and the forwards hat really deserved it. Re- starting the St Helens men again swarmed to the War- rington 25, This success was quickly followed by a dropped goal by Foulkes almost from half way. Hunter restarted play by a good run. St Helens still kept Warrington de- fending. From a scrimmage in front of goal Foulkes made a mark, and Lee put On another goal, greatly to the delight of the spectators. For a miunte or two after this Warring' ton seemed all at sea, and Hallam had his hands full to pre- vent further score. Close on half-time, however. Isher- wood stole away from the scrimmage at his own 25, and after a grand run passed to Dickenson. who scored and reduced the points against the Warringtonians. Half-time —St Helens, 3 goals 1 try (9 points), Warrington 2 trio! (6 points). St Helens started the second hslf, and the excellent form of their forwards was at once showa by an attack on the Warrington goal. The visitors were penalised, but Foulkes failed at an easy place. Burgess removed play by a fine run but his pass was not accepted. Then Warrington attacked hotly. Harris and Ratcliffe both haying tries disallowed. Warrington pressed for some time, and Burgess dribtded almost over from a scrimmage. Foulkes ran the ball out beyond half way. Warrington returned and Dickenson scored after good passing, Isherwood kicked a goal, thus placing the Warrlngtonlans ahead. Warrington were now going well, and Harris scored within the next few minutes Duckworth also scared a smart try, no goal resulting. St Helens now, after these reverses, played up strongly. After a good run by Fairborn. the ball was carried over Warring- ton's line, but the St Helen; men were unable to get over it St Helens had a shot at the Warrington goal, but a free kick by Hallam cleared the danger. The match was exceedingly interesting all through. Isherwood made a grand run, but Foulkes collared. The St Helens forwards dribbled back, but the Warrington men had now the upper hand. Final.— Warrington, 1 goal 5 tries (17 points) ; St Helens, 3 goals 1 try (9 points).

THE NORTHERN LEAGUE.

BRADFORD v. RUNCORN.

This match was played at Park Avenue, before about 7,000 spectators. Bradford played Ward for Boyes, sad B Smales for Feather. A strongish wind blew straight down the ground towards the city, and the home side, winning the toss, rho% to play with the wind. The opening was tame, but in time Murgatroyd made a good run, Bradford getting an attacking position. Houghton's defence was conspicuous, sad though Rradford were mainly on the attack Butterworth had hard lines in nut scoring, The game beataine very keen The first score came to Bradford, Murgatroyd and Sinton making an opening, frcm which Laidlaw scored a try. L ft Wood failing at goal. Runcorn improved after this, but the game was still unpleasantly keen, and a man on either side was Cautioned by the referee. Half-time arrived with the score—Bradford 1 try. Runcorn nil. With the wind with them Runcorn, on the resump- tion began to attack. Players on both sides had been apparently advised to keep their tempers. and Houghton, who at the close of the first half had been kicked in the face, was able to resume. The Cheshire team adopted kicking tactics, and were materially aided by the bad fielding of the Bradford men. There were occasional Spurts on the home side, but at last Sinton had a kick charged, enabling Butterworth to score a soft try, from which Farmer kicked a goal, Jolley shortly after dropped another goal, and Farmer. with the Bradford team well beaten, ran in a try. and placed a goal. Result :—Runcorn 3 goals and 2 tries (12 points), Bradford 1 try (3 points). = This is the experience of Mr. VERNUM, back of Ez: is, New Spring Street, Brookfields, Birmingham.

Agonising pains in the arms, like rheumatism, sent me to a doctor; then I tried hospitals, but all seemed useless. I gradually lost the use of my limbs and could do nothing for myself, not even feed myself. I found I was para- lysed. My wife had confidence in Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People and got some. After four boxes I regained use of my fingers, and then recovery was rapid. I .took twelve boxes altogether and am now well, and can walk twenty miles."

THE MUNICIPAL TRADING

AGITAI lON.

Correspondence between Alderman Monks and Mr, Norbury Williams.

An Inquiry by the Latter Suggested.

November Bth, 1901.

The Editor of the "Examiner."

Sir,—l wrote Mr Norbury Williams last week, taking exception to his remarks at the two meet- ings recently held on the question of " Municipal Trading.' Mr Williams carefully evades my main point, but fills five sheets of paper with questions which, as he wrote them, he would know 1 could not answer. _ _

_ Under the circumstances it will only be a waste ef ;line to continue the correspondence further and so far as I am concerned it is closed. _

Unfortunately I am not at liberty to send Mr Williams' letter, since his telegram this morning reads:—"Correspondence not closed yet.l am reply- ing to you." However, my letters will be understood, and I shall feel obliged by your publishing them in to- morrow's issue. I have no uesire to add anything by way of comment. Yours truly F W MONKS,

Warrington, November 1, 1901. S. Norbury Williams, Esq., Manchester. Dear Sir,-1 venture to write you respecting the agitatina that is being carried on here against members of the Council trading with the Corpora- tion. While agreeing cordially with the underlying principle involved, and the efforts that are being made to improve the status of municipal work, 1 do most strongly object to the methods and lines that have so far been adopted by the leaders of the movement. . .

From the commencement nothing but vague charges have been made, reflecting upon every member of the Council, until they culminated lin your very unfair and. I would add on behalf of those most immediately concerned, very unjust comparison with Manchester. Having been a member of the Council for 11 years,l nave a close and intimate knowledge ot what has been going on, and I not only assert that most of the stacements made, as to illegal trading are entirely without any foundation, but that the work of the Council has been carried on in a way that would secure your commendation rather than censure, if you were fully informed as to the facts. While listening to your speech on Wednesday last 'at the Parr Hall, I felt that you had an imperfect knowledge of fbe circumstances, or you would have spoken very differently. One version is often good until the other is told Even the prisoner at the bar is allowed to make a defence before the judge passes sentence. But so far as I know the leaders of this movement have never yet attempted to hear what independent members of the Council have to say on the other side. You yourself came to a public meeting prompted only by Messrs Dodds, Fleming and Pemberton, who naturally enough state their case , to their own advantage..

I ask you is it fair, is it honourable, for any out- sider, no matter what his experience may have been in other towns.under other Conditions to come here and make statements that reflect upon the character and integrity of the whole of the members of the council without making independent enquir- ies to ascertain bow far the statements are true? The movement here is generally understood to have arisen out of personal quarrels, and certainly has all the appearance of such, notwithstanding the denials made. One naturally hesitates to intervene or allow oneself to be mixed up with a disagreeable matter of this sort, and there has not yet been an opportunity of doing anything without incurring such a risk, or I would have taken some step before now. I know other members of the Council who bare refrained from the same reason. I feel sure there is a large proportion of the members who agree with me, and would heartily welcome any fair and reasonable inquiry, say by yourself, to enable a full statement to be made public; one that would satisfy every ratepayer in the town. I have ventured to wrie you in the hope that you may be able to clear the question of the per- sonalties that have so far prevented the general public from getting at such information as would enable them to form a correct opinion as to the charges made.—Yours truly, F. W. MONKS.

Warrington, November 7th, 1901. S. Norbury Williams, Esq., Manchester. Sir,—Your letter of the sth asks a lot of ques- itons to which I am unable to reply, not having the necessary information nor any means of obtain- ing it. Had you agreed to my suggested enquiry you would have been able to satisfy yourself not only in regard to these particular items but also upon the larger question as to whet ground or reason there is for the y resent agitation. I have nothing to denounce, because I maintain the facts as put forward by you, and your friends, are not a true statement of the case. You evi- dently did not vr6h the public to ges at the truth, feeling possibly the agitation would collapse with- out obtaining the result des;gned by its leaders. On the general question of municipal trading I think I have as high an ideal as amyone and would not hesitate to publicly denounce anything that was illegal or improper, but I am quite unable to follow to the length you seem willing to go as regards trading with limited companies. For instance because Mr Hall is my uncle, and a co- director of Monks, Hall end Co.,Limited, and at the same time a director of J Buckley and Co., Limited,l (of which by the way I was not previously aware) then I must retire from the Council,or the firm of J Buckley and Co.. Limited be debarred from any further trading with the Council. This to me is the height of absurdity. If such a standard is to be adopted then it will almost make municipal govern in ent impossible. The law permits a corporation to enter into con- tracts or trade with all limited companies, notwith- standing that a director, manager or share Colder may be a member of the corporation. I see nothing improper in this. The system may be open to abuse, but what is there on this earth that. is not ? Ihe remedy in my opinion, is not an alteration of the law, but in the opening of the eyes of the public to the possibility of its abuse and the crea- tion and maintenance of such a high moral tone and standard of purity as will prevent any man

endeavouring to use his privileges as a member of the Council to the advantage of himself or his firm. iny movement for attaining these objects will have my cordial and active support. In conclusion, I would only say so far as War- rington is concerned, the council has been singularly free of any attempt at regular or persistent trading on the part of the members, such as has been dis- closed in other towns, and if any illegal trading has taken place at all, it has been so occasional and trivial as to pass unnoticed and without remark. Now that the matter is closed I propose sending my letters to the press for publication and shall be glad if you will telegraph in the morning before 12.0 if I may include yours. Yours truly,,___

F. W:kONKS.

P S.—My father authorizes me to say Mr has not given you a correct version of what passed between them in regard to the position of the Mayor.

1.0 G.T. No. 1 Convention S. W. Lancashire. The quarterly session of the above was held on Saturday in the Co-operative Hall, Leigh. The session was presided over by Bro. G Clarke C Conn. (Earlestown). There was a very: good attend- ance of representatives from Warrington, Earles- town. Widnes, Ashton-in-Makerfield, Leigh, Wigan, St. Helens and other parts of the Convention Area After the opening ceremony, Sister Mrs Firth, V D of Barrow, and Bro E Newman, D,S.J.T. were re- ceived with the usual honours. The C. Secretary Bro Smith, (Ashton) reported the lodges to be doing good work, throughout the convention area, and a new lodge was to be opened in Leigh and one to be re-opened at Ashton. The C.S.JT., Sister Mrs Woods, (Ekileetown), reported on the Juvenile Templars saying that the temples were holding their owu but could not give a full report as all the temples had not sent returns, Bro Turner CB S re- ported on the Brewster Sessions and regretted that magistrates seem to lean to granting licences for the sale of drink to so many football players and hoped this would have the consideration of the meeting. The reports were adopted after some discussion Bro Clarke reported on his visit to War- rington, and hoped to see the lodges in that town once more to the front in Good Templar work. The report was supported by Bro. Sheath (Warrington), who said he hoped in the near future to see the lodge and temple again up and doing, The report was adopted. After the vote of thanks to the Leigh members a very pleasant session was brought to a close, after which the members sat d own to a good tea, A very good meeting followed. The chair was taken by Bro Clarke, D.E.S. Songs and recitations were given by members and friends,and an address was given by Bro Parr.

The number in the corresponding week of last year was 93. showing a de- crease of 19, being a net decrease in 1901, to date, of 183.

MODEST REQUEST.—There are some disadvan- tages which attach themselves to persons who have become famous. These, however, often have their ludicrous side. It is said that at one tune Henry Clay, the American statesman, was travelling in the West, and stopped overnight in a little log cabin inhabited by an old man and his wife. After breakfast the next morning, his old host, who had been in a flutter of excitement ever since he learned who his distinguished guest was, said he would like to make one slight request before the visitor de- parted. "Couldn't ye," he said, with evident anxiety—" couldn't ye jest make my wife an' me a little speech before leavin' us?"

of inviting the Corporation to attend " Divine Service with him on Mayor's Sunday both at the Parish Church and at Wycliffe Congregational Church." As the Minister of ths latter place of Worship, it is perhaps fitting that I should point out that what Mr Alderman Wright really did in the matter of the Corporation visit to Wycliffe was not on Mayor's Sunday, but on the occasion of the re-opening of the Church after renovation. The pro- cedure of the double attendance at Worship on Mayor's Sunday was adopted by the late Mayor, Mr Alderman Roberts, who having invited the Council Ito attend his own Church at the morning service, I invited them also to attend with him at the Parish Church in the evening. Mr. Roberts thus manifested the fact that although, as a Nonconformist, he gave honour to his own Church on the Mayor's Sunday, he desired to show respect also to the Church to which so large a number of the Council were attached. Thus Mr. Alderman Roberts showed his agreement with the late Alderman Wright, who himself declared that he wished to be looked upoa as Mayor of Warrington, and not Mayor of any political party ortreligious sect.l may perhaps be allowed to speak with sincere appreciation of this broad and Catholic spirit, and oo express regret that our new Mayor has elected to inaugurate his mayoralty upon narrower and more sectarian lines. His decision, however, occasioned me no surprise, but none the less I very sincerely deplore it.—Yours faith- fully,

JOHN YONGE,

Runcorn School Board Affairs. tSir,—The highly contentious character of the election b'udget which the Chairman of the School Board sprung on the last meeting and the strangling of its discussion s my excuse for now submitting the progressive side of the question. To deal with expenditure alone excites the suspicion that the acconnts have been ransacked in desperation ter some data to support the cry of " Down with the rates." The one is as misleading as the other was insincere. Reduced expenditure may mean sacrifice of income, and its ex porte use has no place in business finance. The true test of efficiency and economy is what must the ratepayer find to make up the difference between income and expenditure. The actual figures from the audited accounts after regulating bank balances and everything calculated to confuse, show : Progressive for three years, average £1,807 153 6d per annum, 7.1c1 in the £. Clerical for three years £1,941 5s 93 per annum, 811. in the £, (taking Mr Imison's basis of id yielding £234 103 id.) The Clerical Board have taken £1,200 more from the rates than their Progressive predecessors. £4OO 103 6d , of this has gone in actual increased expenditure, and I the rest is suck ill a re-arzangement of balances more profitable to Pores Banking Company than advan- tageous to the ratepayers. Could the collapse of the "Down with the Rates" party be more disastrous ? The Chairman's claim for the credit of establishing the Pupil Teachers' Centre must be disallowed. The fact Is that this magnificent project was blocked for 12 years because hid party refused to pay their share of the cmt. I call special attention to the fact that at no time has the School Board expenditure touched the 91 of which the Chairman's party has made such unfair capital. This attempt to exaggerate the cost of the School Board education is only bolstered upby appeals to the " back cf the poor rate" and other misleading and irrelevant data. Faithfully ycurs, Lyndburst,

FRED JOHN NORMAN. Runcorn, November 11, 1901.

--- Sir.—The proceedings at the Runcorn School Board last Thursday may not have been very edifying but they were distinctly instructive. The Vicar's usual strategy deserted him and he appeared as he IS. To use a homely phrase he let the cat oat of the nag. We now know where and what he is on the School Board. He Is not May chairman but, according to his own des- cription. he is there as Vicar of the parish and from beneath his canonical garments he has allowed the cloven hoof of the sectarian to appear where there should be neither sect nor party. Plain Alfred Maitland Wood will no longer describe him in School Board affairs. By his own admission he sits on the Board and controls its affairs as Vicar of Runcorn, Canon of Chester Cathedral, Chairman of the Pariah School Managers. and member of the Arch- bishops and the Diocesan Education Committees. What does this mean ? That he is the avowed enemy of the Board Schools of which the ratepayers have foolishly ' given him the overseership. As a priest of the high " church school, the Church and her organis- ations have first place in bis affections. And to forward what he considers her interests he would use the"strategy of truth" or any similar weapon that would serve his ends. Why has not the Conserva- tive party in Runcorn a local habitation in addition to a name. Ask any member of the inner circle of the party why the promising scheme for,building a club in Greenway-road fell through and the answer will aston- ish. The phrase "strategy of truth" is the very essence of the practice of the Jesuits. Mr Norman was very gentle with the canonical sophist or he would have boldly declared that the phrase "strategy of truth" was introduced into Runcorn by the rev gentleman himself. It was after tbe last School Board election. The Church party had gathered in the Parish Schools to shake hands with each other on their glorious victory. Pride of conquest had made the Vicar reck- less and he announced to an audience bubbling over with glee, that the fight had been won by confounding the people with the" strategy of truth." If a general sets a nurc her of dummy soldiers on a hill to give the enemy the impression that be is holding it in strength and then takes his real army a circuitous way to strike his foes in the rear, that is strategy. Strategy is a trick, deceit, imposition, and Mr Norman's charge is that the Church party, led by the Canon, used facts in such a way as to deceive the ratepayers and cause them to receive wrong impressions. To those who can read between the lines and who can strip the discussion of its verbiage to reach the real essence, Mr Norman proved his point. What did the ratepayers understand by "Down with the rates." That the vicar and his followers if put in office would reduce the amount of the rate demanded from them. Did the vicar and Co. mean this? They knew as every educstionist must know that they could not reduce the rates without at the same time reducing the equipment of the Board Schools and the efficiency of the teaching staff, and this was more than hey dare attempt to do. The spirit was willing hut he flesh was weak. Now when they have to ag amn ppear before the tribunal of the ratepayers with an in- reased rate, they have to plrad "guilty" to n ot carrying cut their promise, and ask their judges to believe, in extenuation,that they have done more work for the money. Will the ratepayers accept such an ntyc opsehhelei sh eodf itnh tehve .0 En txaarmy sncehr o airne sllpluenn: eicuse backed by not a tittle of evidence? stthr aa ntegayn eotfhterruitth t hla ft the there daiys coTrnh,erebceanlat onedi d etvhiidneennmesoreofeertthaein" schools reduce the cost of &maintaining the Sunday 1 ,chools by 75 per cent. ; although this is net shown on the face of the balance sheets. If the captain wanted to ride about, said the owner, he must pay for the luxury hirnsslf. Another voyase; again the captain rendered his expenses. " Ab," said the owner, "now this is a proper account ;.there are no buggies there." " No," retorted the captain, "You may not see them, but they are there all the same." FRIDAY NOVEMBER 15, 1901

LATEST NEWS.

The Recent Gate.

Terrible Destruction of Shipping.

The great gale of Tuesday and Wednesday has re- sulted in 37 vessels being wrecked with a total loss of life which is appalling. The number may yet be in- creased as telegrams are still :coming in.

Lifeboat Capsized at Yarmouth.

Nine Men Drowned.

A Yarmouth correspondent, telegraphing yesterday says:—The life boat Beachamp was struk by a sea while returning to Caistor this morning. She turned over,and the crew were imprisoned beneath, but parties of men from the shore plunged into the water up to their necks and rescued three men named Walter Hubbard, Charles Knights, and Jack Hubbard in an unconscious condi- tion. The bodies of eight men have been washed ashore. The total number drowned is nine. Nearly all the drowned leave large families. The rescued men, who have been cmveyed to their homes, are all expected to recover from the effect sof their immersion. The Bea- champ was proceeding to the rescue of a Lowestoft smack, w hich went on the Barber sand, but which got off safely.

A Famous Jockey Injured,

Serious Accident to Sam Loates.

Whilst riding at Northampton races yesterday after- noon Sam Loates, the well known and popular jockey, met with a seriou s accident, breaking his thigh and several ribs, besides a bad scalp wound. He was at once removed to the Northampton Infirmary, and the doctors describe his condition as very serious.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT],

In a little Over a week the electorate will have to decide whether or not the Sclwo' Board is to be governed for the next three years by the clerical party. Already there is every indication that the struggle will be keen and bitter. During the past fortnight there has been at work for both parties quite an army of canvassers, who, armed with mural literature, have solicited from door to door , the support of the elector. Both sides have not confined themselves to mere canvassing. The Cleri- cal party have issued since last Saturday nigh on a dozen different handbills which contain some very pertinent queries: On the other hand the Progres- sives have billed the town with their watch cry pretty extensively. Public meetings have been held by both parties,which have been exceptionally well -attended. On Friday the Progressives had a capital] meeting in Brunswick Schoolroom,but on Tuesday the Voluntaryist party had a meeting quite enthusiastic in its character. For the last week there has been held in each ward a series of viewson the education question on behalf of the Progressites. Mr J M Frith was the lectur- er and the lantern was in charge of Mr J H Pick- thall. Both sides are working with desperate energy and whichever side wins it will be by but a small majority.

, Another correspondent writes Now that winter has arrived once more the ques- tion arises in the minds of a certain class of people as to how they are to spend the winter evenings; To men who after a bard day's work can spend their time in the quiet of their homes or those people who are of a studious turn of mind and en- joy themselves poring over books this is a question aasily answered, but I write about more especially the young man in "diggings." What a magnificent choice he has! There are three places for him to go to, the theatre,readin-• room or bar-parlour and oftener than not it is the last place. The Liberal club is also open to men of Liberal views. Cannot the Council do something for these young men? A body that can spend thousands on a bridge and park ought surely to be able to see its way to spend a hundred or two on a room or rooms wnere young men could congregate and have a smoke and a game of chess, draughts or dominoes, &c. Runcorn during the winter months is enough to drive any young man insane almost.

-- I would like to call the attention of the Free Library Committee to the reading-room. This mat- ter was first pointed out to me by a local gentle- man of high repute and I went down to see for myself. As soon as I got through the door I found out that what he had told me was quite correct. The etench was something awful. The room is degenerating into (especially during the day time) a lounging place for loafers and cornertuen and I am afraid that the majority of them belong to the "great unwashed' and *a bath would do them no harm. I have repeatedly gone in during the day time and found a drunken man asleep and certainly this is not an altogether welcome addition. Can- not arrangements be made for a policeman to visit the room occasionally, and remove superfluous " drunks " and loafers?

19, VVilson-Patten-Arpet, November 14, 1901.

Corporation Contracts.

Sir,— At the Parr Hall meeting last week something was said abcue my connection with H. Ingram,Limitedai who arc putting electrin fittings in the new Police Court. It is quite true that lam a seareh older in the company,l have £5O invested in it. Now assuming that the company pay 10 per cent. I shall receive £5 by way of dividend in the course of the next few months. Of that £5 probably a few shillings will have been derived from the Police Court contract. My member- ship of the Corporation costs me, roughly, £25 a year. Ileave you- readers to judge whether I can be said to have joined the Council, and to keep my seat from Impure motives. I may add that I have owned the shares only some seven or eight months ; that I had nothing whatever to do with the placing of the contract, which was obtained in open com- petition ; and that not a single member of the Committee who awarded it was aware that I had anything to do with H Ingram, Limited. I know quite well that all this has nothing to do with the rights and wrongs of municipal trading, but it has everything to do with the question of one's motives in sitting in the Council. The loudest tongued advocate of the latest local fad would admit that a man would be a fool to pay £25 to secure five shillings. But I am not writing to defend trading between limited companies whose shareholders are Town Cone- cillors and the Corporation. In my opinion it needs no defence where all is open and above board and contracts are entered into on terms advantageous to the rats:- payers. It is legal, and lam not persuaded that it is not moral,—that depends on the way it is carried out ; train my own personal knowledge I can confidently affiim that not a shadow of a shade of suspicion attaches to the letting of these contracts here. Finally, it is certainly expedient for if it were abolished the area of competition would be restricted and trade would be driven out of the town. S I shall not sell my shares and I shall not resign my seat unless and until my own constituents demand it. Net all the impertinences of Manchester, nor the irrelevances of Warrington, orators will make me budge. The object of this letter is not to discuss my connec- tion with H Ingram, Limited, but to point out to your readers the evidence which the incident at the Parr Hall affords of bad faith on the part of some at least of those who have had a share in the recent and—as I believe— expiring agitation in which these gentlemen have con- trived to impress the imagination at the public with the purity of their motives, whilst vehemently repro- bating the motives of others. In the first place your readers will remember that Mr Pemberton, in' announcing my connection witn Ingrams was careful to add that I was the Mayor's brother (though why I should not be allowed to stand on my own feet I cannot imagine. no" consolidation" having yet taken place in our case), and that the Secretary was the Mayor's brother-in-law. Why these marked refer- ences to the Mayor in a matter with which he never had anythinglto do The Mayor is not,and never has been a shareholder in Ingrams, and I question whether he knew, until the Parr Hall meeting, that I was. Does this look like " purity "for purity's sake, or " purity" as a stick to beat the Mayor with ? But my second point is still more important. The information that I was a shareholder in Ingrams was obtained from me by Mr Councillor Forshaw, and by him supplied to Mr Pemberton for use at the meeting. Mr Forshaw's clerk inspected the register at my office on the morning of the meeting. Now listen to this :—Mr Forshaw has for years, was at the date of the Parr Hall meetiug, and is at this moment the holder of snares of the market value of about £1,300 in Fletcher Russell's, Limited, who in this present year and up to the end of September only have executed contracts with the Corporation of the value of £6lO. While therefore Mr. Forshaw was hold- ing me up to scorn, me, toe holder of 50 £1 shares in Ingram, he was humourously enough, keeping the fact to himself that he was a sinner to 20 times the extent that I was! This is funny, but it 35 not all. It was Mr. Forshaw who got the Council to pass a resolution , not to trade with members of the Council or their part- ners—a singular superfluous affair seeing that that very thing is expressly forbidden by statute. Forther.Mr For- thaw's name was one of the seven given by Mr Norbury Williams as those retiring councillors who had promised not to support " direct or indirect trading with the Council. either by members privately or as shareholders in limited companies." When challenged at the meet- ing to deny this Mr Forshaw, who was present, re- mained silent. Lastly,Mr Forshaw has in various ways given the agitators considerable help. Where is the bo,?a fides in all this ? Have I not established my contention that much, at all events, of the present agitation has an object very different from that which has been its rallying cry ? I have no manner of doubt that had I not been the Mayor's brother— a relationship which I am still proud to own—no mention would have been made of my shares in Ingrams, Limited. A much more effective weapon was at hand, but it was not used. Why ? Lt the public judge.— Yours truly, F R ROBERTS. Warrington, 7th November, 1901.

Sir' —The question of municipal trading cannot I think be treated in an off hand sort ef way. The Chairman of the Parr Hall meeting asked for the "Town Council to refuse to re-elect any Alderman who has been or is now engaged in trading with the Coun- cil." To draw any hard and fast rule as is here advo- cated would I fear exclude from all town councils and public bides some of our best men, of the highest character and business qualifications, who are will- ing to place a large and varied experience at the service of the ratepsyerw. But 013 A is led to ask here is:the course suggested to be strictly confined to muni- cipal trading or does the question which has been raised only touch the fringe of the subject? For a moment let us look at the principle which Mr Dodds has attacked from another point of view. Not very long ago there sere connected with the firm of which Mr Dodds is secretary three well known public men. The late Mr Pearson was the chairman of one of our great railways. When he was Mayor of Liverpool a better occupant never sat in tho civic chair than Alder- man John Pearson. The late Mr Bleckly took a public interest in questionS connected with the Local Govern- ment Board for the County of Lancashire, and the late Mr KnowleS, lal.j'., was a director of the London and North-Western Railway Company. Mr Pearson, as Chairman of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway, it goes without saying had great influence, and during his chairmanship we find the Warrioron'firm with which be was identified, executing large contrasts, directly or indirectly, for Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway purpos2s. I state a simple fact and will not enlarge upon it at the present time. Again the late Mr Henry Sleekly who was much interested in the County's administration attended its Meetings and was considered by many an authority in these matters but though serving the public in this way the Warrington firm supplied, directly or indirectly,tons of ironwork in connection with the enlargement of one at least of the county asylums in the Palatine. Now does saenssyone suppose that if these far seeing level-headed in gentlemen had been living, their course of conduct as public men ill relation to what is here given would have changed ? Perhaps it is a big order to say one way or the other, and as the writer is only jost !little less than his clothes and without the insight which I some good people claim, an acknowledgement must be made ol one's inability to speak with any degree of cer- tainty on the point. But I should like to know where :the; wrong or " immorality " Comes in if it cannot be shown or proved that influence and position are used for personal gain, and what the difference is between this kind of trading and trading with a corpor- ation by its aldermen and councillors ? If I may so put it, I have an idea that this matter should be surveyed from a broad standpoint for ills only by so doing that we can aim at being anything like practical. Mr. Dodds is reported to have said "Don't mistake me ; lam not 50 sanguine as to suppose that we shall root out this evil in a day, or a year, or two or three years." Again I would ask is this language to refer only to municipal trading,or must it cover a wider area and apply to other spheres of trading ? Mr. Dodds' reply to these ques- tions will be awaited with interest. As a ratepayer,and one having no " axe to grind " in tl.,e business, I think the electors of Latchfnrd Ward are to be congratulated in again returning to the Town Council Mr. Ellison.— Yours Sec., A.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By " ()valise

Warrington had their first rest from Northern League football on Saturday when they met St. Helens under the auspices of the South West Lancashire Competi- tion which thus provides that old rivals like Warring- ton, Runcorn, St. Helens and Widnes shall not be debarred from meeting one another though two of them have been promoted to the Senior League. 'Twould indeed be a pity if these matches, so fraught with keen rivalry and determination on the part of con- testants, were to lapse from any cause. South Lan- cashire people are more interested in the doings of South Lancashire clubs than they are in the perform- ances and abilities of even Oldham, Swinton or Brough- ton. For years they have struggled for superiority and lam glad indeed that, under the South West Lanca- shire Competition they can still tilt at one another with something at stake.

The admission of Warrington to the Northern League did not, of itself, guarantee superiority over the clubs left out, and I was, therefore, interested in seeing how they would shape against St. Helens, who are perform- ing only moderately in their own class. I certainly expected Warrington to prove the better side, because they are better now than they have been for a long time, and I was not disappointed, though the losers made a plucky fight of it from start to finish. The gate seemed just about on a par with what it always was when War- rington visited Knowsley-road,aud the conditions gener- ally were just the same as of yore. Even the composi- tion of the teams had changed very little, and the 1 players were mostly known to each other from past experience.

There was half a gale blowing at Knowsley-road, and when Warrington won the toss they were set to face the breeze. Right from the kick-off they made pro- gress, and in the first minute Isherwood scored a smart try after Harris had kicked across from touch. Then it was a case of St. Helens first and Warrington nowhere for some time. Taking advantage of the strong wind the Saints kept Warrington on the defen- sive, and by strong rushing play they gave Isherwood and his men a warm time. Attack after attack was repulsed, but at last the persistence of the home team was rewarded, Hunter scoring a try on his own. Lee converted and placed St. Helens ahead. Warrington made occasional bursts to the other end by good passing runs, but it was almost impossible to do more than defend owing to the wind. St. Helens certainly made the most of it, and succeeded in raising their score to 9 points by the aid of a couple more goals. Just before the interval Isherwood got away, and after a splendid run the length of the field gave Dickenson an easy try which reduced the homesters' lead very much.

Being now only three points in arrears Warrington rather fancied their chances on changing ends. They very soon attacked and went in for the quick passing game which always tells against an inferior club. Harris claimed a try as the result of a smart follow-up but Referee Priestley was against him, and a few minutes later he disallowed an apparently fair try by Ratcliffe. These mishaps made things look serious for Warrington especially as the Saints were playing with dogged determination. Combination eventually pre- vailed and Dickenson cleverly put the finishing touch on a good passing run, Isherwood practically settling the result of the match by placing a goal. Warring- ton were all over the home team after this, though the wind had fallen away to I nothing almost. Duckworth stole over very smartly from a " scrum " and Harris's speed gained him a try after one of the Saints had kicked over his own line. Fast play continued to the end when Warrington had won by 1 goal 5 tries (17 points) ; to 3 goals 1 try (9 points).

In many respects the game was an excellent one. The wind spoiled the play very much in the first half, but the game was always so keenly:contested that inter- est never flagged. Then, too, the Warrington backs were in form, and gave a display which did them infin- ite credit. Isherwood was in excellent form and played, whatll certainly consider,his best game this season,while Dickenson ably supported him in every move. The two centres were very strong either on the attack or defence while Harris and Ratcliffe always made progress when in possession of the ball. At half.back Burgess and Duckworth played an uneven game, occasionally doing something clever, and then muffing the next moment. One was about as good as the other, but they undoubt- ' edly filled the places of Allen and Hockenhall very creditably. If Burgess would only pass when he sees that he cannot possibly get through he would be a more valuable man than he is even now. Hallam and Foulkes were about on a par at full back, while in the forward argument St Helens were a bit the best, especially in fast work.

The St. Helens team is far from being a bad one. Combination and speed are not its best points, but there is plenty of energy all round, and individually the players can give a good account of themselves. Hunter and Lee are a very fair pair of centres, but the wings, Doherty and Fairburn seem to have seen their best days. The latter made a poor show considering the reputation he was reputed to have with Hull Kings- ton Rovers.

The West Lancashire League, table up-to-date is as Follows Pointe.

In the Northern League games Broughton added another to their long list of successes by severely thrashing Hunslet. The latter won forward but in the rear were hopelessly out-manceuvred by the speedy Rangers, who scored. 23 points to 2 points. Oldham went down at Hull, and it is worth noting that they have never looked up since Warrington spoiled their sequence of successes, at Wilderspool. Leigh made a good fight at Huddersfield, but should not have allowed 18 points to be piled on against them in the first half.

A good performance was accomplished by Runcorn at Bradford, for after looking likely to lose they got ahead in the second portion and finally ran the heavy Bradfordians off their feet with three goals and two tries to a try. Salford had an easy win at Brighouse, while Swinton found no great difficulty about beating Halifax. Cooper scored three tries for them. The League table up to date is as follows :

Harriers.

Warrington Athletic Club had Runcorn Harriers as visitors last Saturday. Runcorn had a very poor team, only three runners turning up, whilst the home club had. 13 out. A mile break was agreed upon, and the pack proceeded across Arpley fields, Walton Arches,And round by Acton Grange. After having run about four miles, the pack formed up for the mile break. C straw and J Mather immediately broke away, followed 'by Price and two members of Runcorn. A grand struggle took place for first position, J Mather just winning from C Straw. The race resulted in a win for Warring- ton by eleven points. Position: of counters, Warrington A.C. :—J Mather, 1" ; C Straw, 2 ; W Price, 3 ; total, six points. Runcorn H. ; W Hulse, 4 ; J Povey, 6 ; T Povey, 7; total, 17 points. Next Saturday's fixture is with Sef ton Harriers, at Wavertree.

About thirty members of the Sankey club turned up at headquarters, the Sloop Hotel, on Saturday, to indulge in an ordinary run. Two packs were turned out, the slow preceding the fast by about ten minutes. Seven miles of good country were traversed by way of the Bold estate, the slow pack keeping well in front. The first to arrive was J Potts, followed by F Cowap and Parsonage, jun., all" slows." The fast pack soon followed, T Devlin, S Parsonage, and E Green finishing at a smart pace.

BARROW V. BARLESTOWN•

At Barrow: Barrow started with the wind. Findley kicked well up, and Walker centred finely, the Barlestown custodian saving from Cunningham. Docherty saved a rush by Barlestown, Play favoured the home team. Boss played nicely, but play went to the other end. Barrow kept well up, Fyfe and Boss playing well. Barlestown transferred, but Duguid saved. Quarter-time—no score, Barrow now attacked strongly. Walker eventually kicked over after Clew bed saved several shots. A swift run down by Bariestown was checked by Docherty. From a free kick Cunningham scored, but It was disallowed. A foul against Sanders gave the visitors relief, and a smart was sent in by Bills. Barrow I tran ferred, and M'Farlane putting beautifully in, Hall scored. Cunningham scored and Barlestown scored. Half- time—Barrow 3,Bariestown 1. Final—Barrow 6,Bariestown3.

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD ELECTION. TO THE RATEPAYERS OF RUNCORN. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN,— In seeking your support at the coming election I beg to say that I am not opposed to any system of educa- tion wIIICH IS THOROUGH AND FAIR TO ILL THE RATV- DLYERS. To give the best education possible to the rising Reneration is the most sacred duty of all parents and gnardians. School., then, and what is taught in them should be their choosing Some may choose Board Schools and others Denomi- national Schools, but those who choose Hoard Schools should pay for them. It is unjust that ratepayers who, tor many reasons prefer Denominational Schools, should be forced to pay for those schools of which they do not approve, whilst they must support the Denominational Schools which they prefer. Whilst promising you the services of whatever abilities I may possess in the cause of education I hereby declare th%t I shall oppose on principle the increase of rates for the one-sided system which now exists. I have the honour to be Your obedient servant, 3 CHAMBBRSL_

Our opponents, the Sectarian party. cbtained control of the Board by promising to " Down with the rates," and have instead increased your burden by £1,200 in threa years. We are anxious to give justice and fair play to Board School and Voluntary School alike. The accusation that, le If elected, we should close Voluntary Schools is as absurd i as it is false. We have neither the power nor the disposi- tion to interfere with their work in any way. We , cannot close Voluntary Schools. , and it is of vital importance that the School Boards A determined attack is being made by Sectarians upon the Board School system, with a view to its extinction. The situation at present Is one of great peril , should be controlled by broad minded lay-men who will t- not sacrifice the educational interests of the nation to . any sectarian influence. In education we know no sect:nor party. Our sole aim is to provide a sound education for every child, accom- panied by Biblical teaching , free from any attempt to 4 viola•m the religious convialons of parents. support -` . COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON LANCA- SHIRE COUNTY (LUNATIC ASYLUMS). At a Special Meeting of the Council of the Borough of Warrington held on the 9th day of November, 1901 at the Town Hall pursuant to the Borough Funds Act,1872, the following resolution was passed by an absolute majority of the Council That the Council do jointly with other Lancashire County Boroughs promote during the ensuing Session of Parliament. " A Bill to alter and amend the Lancashire County (Lunatic Asylums and other powers) Act, 1891, and to alter the mode of contribution to the expenses of the Lancashire Asylums Board" and that the costs and expenses attending the same shall be chargeable upon and payable out of the Borough Fund and that the Council are hereby authorised under and in pursuance of" Ao Act to authorise the application of funds of Municipal Corporations and other governing bodies in 'certain cases "to apply such Borough Fund to the pay. ment of such costs and expenses accordingly. DATED this 15th day of November 1901. J. LYON WHITTLE, Town Clerk. Town Ball, Warrington. • I

Hull maintained constant pressure, Lees striving hard to make ground, but got little support, The.flnal sc tres read : —Hull, one goal and two tries (eight points) ; Oldham, one goal (two points). BROUGHTON RANGERS V HUNSLET. Great interest was centred In the meeting of these two clubs, owing to the greatly improved form of Hunsiet, and about 9,000 spectators assembled on the Rangers' ground to witness the encounter. Since the beginning of October Hunsiet have met and defeated Leigh, Warrington, and Swinton. and as the Rangers were still undefeated, it was expected that a very stubbornly contested game would result Unfortunately for Hunslet, they were without W Goldthorpe and Hannah. and their absence severely weakened the com- bination of their three-quarters. Independent of these absences, however, the visitors gave a very feeble display, compared with that of the Rangers, and they were decisively beaten by the large margin of twenty-one points. From the commencement of the game the Rangers showed far superior form, and their backs quickly got going. with the result that Huoslet were severely pressed. W James placed a penalty goal for the Rangers. This was quickly followed by a very fine try by S James, which W James improved: Hogg shortly afterwards scored behind the posts, from which W James placed another goal, and at half-time the Rangers led by twelve points. The second half was almost all in favour of the Rangers. Their backs combined and passed spiendlilly. and Hunslet were completely overplayed. Wilson. White- head. and Hogg scored tries, one of which W James improved. A. Goldthorpe kicked a penalty goal for Hunsiet, and the game ended :—Broughton Rangers, four goals and five tries (23 points) ; Hunslet, one goal (two points).
